PREFACE 

In recent years radical changes in both content and method have been made in the teaching of bookkeeping. The traditional approach through the journal has been generally discarded. Definition of terms and rules of debit and credit that formerly characterized the beginning of the study of a topic are now reached in the conclusion of the lesson. Forms with which the student was confronted without preparation are now carefully developed. The endless succession of long practice sets has yielded to the short narrative that can be completed in one or two recitations. These are some of the drastic changes that have come about very largely through a realization that the study of bookkeeping must be made rational, leading to a development of the faculty of interpreting business facts correctly and to an appreciation of the principles and practices by which prudent men are governed in the management of their business affairs. 

This conception of bookkeeping as an interpretation and an analysis of business situations has been stressed by the authors in the hope that this book will find a greater usefulness than merely that of serving as a technical treatise for the student preparing for the vocation of bookkeeping. In a larger sense, the study of bookkeeping should develop in the student habits of order and clear thinking, should stimulate his imagination and intelligent initiative, and should inculcate sound concepts and lofty ideals in business, irrespective of whether all this preparation will seek expression in the field of bookkeeping, secretarial work, salesmanship, advertising, or business management. 

This book had its inception in the need that was felt by teachers for an elementary text whose content and method of treatment are based on sound pedagogical principles and adapted to the mental capacity of the high school student. The subject matter has been treated topically and divided into chapters, each of which deals with a related group of topics. A distinctive feature of the text is the subdivision of each chapter into lesson units to meet the requirements of the class recitation. Careful gradation and logical sequence have been constantly kept in mind in planning the succession of topics. In nearly every case, the lessons have been made short enough to furnish a complete working unit for a recitation period and yet sufficiently comprehensive to present a logical development of the topic and to supply an adequate quantity of drill and problem material. 

Because bookkeeping is of value only as it serves as a means to an end, each topic is presented as an outgrowth of a need created by the demands of a business situation or arising from the inadequacy of a bookkeeping form previously studied. Motivation of this sort is essential if instead of leading to an overemphasis on the routine and mechanical character of the work through the excessive application of principles and procedures, instruction in bookkeeping is to set in motion the mental processes of the student. However, the importance 

III

IV 

PREFACE 

of perfecting the mechanical skills, making response to routine types of transactions automatic, and developing a sense of order, neatness, and accuracy must not be underestimated. There is a definite place in bookkeeping instruction for training in the art of keeping records. 

No efforts have been spared in including in the text models of forms and arrangement so that the student, by imitation, might attain a high standard of workmanship and skill. 

The contention is sometimes made that the topical method of studying bookkeeping results in a set of detached and incoherent ideas. Several precautions have been taken to forestall this criticism. 

In addition to the consideration given to the logical organization of subject matter, each chapter is made to conclude with a set of review questions and problems or with a practice set, in many cases with both, embracing all the principles presented in the chapter. Two extended business practice sets are given in Chapters IX and XIII respectively, in which the entire ground from the beginning is again covered. The incoming and the outgoing papers accompanying the transactions have been prepared for these two sets and are available to those who choose to use them. The authors while favoring the short set as a teaching device are convinced that an occasional long set is valuable for unifying the work and for developing the power of sustained attention. 

Moreover, they advocate the study and the use of business papers somewhere in the course in co-ordination with the bookkeeping records, so that a complete correlation between bookkeeping and business procedure may be established. 

Throughout the book, the fundamental proprietary equation has been stressed as the foundation on which the entire bookkeeping structure rests. The balance sheet, which is commonly used as the approach to the study of accounts, is a bookkeeping expression or translation of the formula "things owned 'things owned ownership.' Every business event changes the aspect of this equation and every bookkeeping entry expresses the changes. The introduction of the fundamental equation, and both the analysis of transactions and the interpretation of the records in terms of the fundamental equation constitute the central idea around which the book has been written. 

Grateful acknowledgment is made to the teachers and the business men who offered many practical suggestions in the preparation of this textbook. The authors and publishers are especially indebted to Mr. 

Frank P. Boltz, Chairman of the Accounting Department, Evander 

Childs High School, New York, for his extremely helpful criticism, to Mr. John R. Byland, Head of the Commercial Department, Hyde 

Park High School, Chicago, Illinois, for his constructive review of the manuscript, to Mr. Louis Braverman, Chairman of the Accounting 

Department, Samuel J. MODERN BOOKKEEPING PRACTICE CHAPTER I  1. ASSETS What Is Meant by "Doing Business"? 

Man has vital wants to satisfy, such as wants of food, shelter, clothing, recreation, etc. The things that satisfy these wants are called goods. Some goods exist in unlimited quantity and are readily available to all. For this reason they are known as free goods; examples are air, sunlight, and in some instances, water. Other goods do not exist in quantities sufficient to meet all wants for them. They are acquired through the help of nature and the effort of man. Such goods are known as economic goods; examples are steel, wheat, cloth, electricity, etc. No man possesses all the economic goods necessary to satisfy all his wants. To meet his needs, he is obliged to exchange some of his own goods for those belonging to others. This exchange of property constitutes the act of doing business.

Bookkeeping Terms Defined 

The act of doing business is performed, for example, when one pays cash for the purchase of an article or for the use of a service. Thus, if Mrs. Smith spends five cents to reach a department store and buys a dress paying $15 cash, she performs two distinct business acts, one the exchange of money for transportation, and the other the exchange of money for a dress. The performance of the act of doing business 1

is known as transacting business, and the act itself is referred to as a business transaction. To transact business, one must possess things that have exchange value. The things one owns that have exchange value are known in bookkeeping as assets. The following itemized statement shows the assets of an individual. 

Arthur Burbank

STATEMENT OF ASSETS 

January 1, 1932 

Cash on hand... $ 75.40

Savings Account 

2046.50 

Household Equipment 

2575.00 Clothes 185.00

Books 135.00 Automobilę 1200.00

Sundry Articles 

60.00 

Total Assets 

$6276.90

Summary 

1. Things that satisfy wants are known as goods. 

2. Goods are either free or economic. 

3. The exchange of economic goods constitutes a business transaction. 

4. Assets are things owned that have money value.

2. GROUPING OF ASSETS Purpose of Classifying Assets 

To operate a business, it is necessary to employ various kinds of assets, such as money, equipment, merchandise, etc. 

These are the working and trading assets of the business. 

Every business act practically causes a change in one or more of these assets. A purchase of goods for cash, for instance, produces an increase in the asset merchandise, and a decrease in the asset cash. It is the business of the bookkeeper to devise methods of keeping account of each asset. To simplify the record keeping, assets are grouped according to some common characteristic and are reported under common heads. 

Classification of Assets 

The following classification includes some of the more common assets, others being postponed for special treatment in later chapters. 

1. CASH. Under this head are included money, checks, postal money orders, express money orders, and bank drafts. 

2. MERCHANDISE includes all the goods and wares purchased for the purpose of resale at a profit. A dealer in hardware would list under this head tools, nails, piping, locks, paints, wire, sheet metal, screening, etc. 

3. REAL ESTATE. This embraces land and buildings owned by the business. 

4. FURNITURE AND FIXTURES comprises all the permanent equipment used in the workroom, storeroom, salesroom and office. Under this head come shelving, counters, showcases, display racks, lighting fixtures, cash registers, workbenches, desks, chairs, tables, safe, typewriters, filing cabinets, etc. 

5. DELIVERY EQUIPMENT includes automobile trucks and cars, wagons, horses, harness, etc.

GROUPING OF ASSETS 

5. 

6. . Under this head are included various supplies and types of service necessary for conducting the business. Common examples of supplies are wrapping paper, postage stamps, stationery, gasoline, etc. Supplies have two characteristics by which they are distinguished from other assets. First, supplies are materials purchased to be used up, whereas merchandise represents goods purchased by the business to be sold at a profit. Paper bags used by a grocer would be considered by him as supplies; but on the books of the paper house that supplied the grocer, they would be classified as merchandise. Secondly, there is generally complete consumption of the portion of the supply used. In this respect supplies differ from furniture and fixtures and from delivery equipment which last for a long time. An automobile truck, for example, would be classified under the head of delivery equipment, whereas the gasoline and oil purchased for it would be treated as supplies. 

Services required by the business may be furnished either by the employees of the business or by some outside agency. 

Common illustrations are: salaries, which represents the cost of services rendered by employees; rent, which represents the cost of space; telephone, which represents the cost of a type of communication; freight, which represents the cost of transporting goods by the railroad, etc. 

Summary 

1. For the purpose of keeping the records of the assets of a business, assets are classified under various heads, such as Cash, Merchandise, Real Estate, Furniture and Fixtures, Delivery Equipment, Supplies and Services, etc. 

2. Merchandise represents goods purchased for the purpose of resale at a profit. Supplies represents material purchased to be consumed by the business. 

3. Certain assets like real estate, furniture and fixtures, and delivery equipment last for a long time. Supplies disappear rapidly as they are used.

EXERCISES

3. CAPITAL The Fundamental Capital Equation 

On February 1, 19—, James Carson goes into business with a cash investment of $2000. This statement implies first, that the asset invested in the business amounts to $2000, and secondly, that Mr. Carson, as the proprietor, has an ownership in this asset. These two ideas may be expressed in the form of an equation. Thus, Cash $2000 = J. Carson's Ownership $2000

In bookkeeping, ownership is referred to as proprietorship or capital. The equation may, therefore, be restated as follows: 

Cash $2000 = J. Carson's Capital $2000 

This equation is known as the fundamental capital equation. Assets and capital are its two fundamental elements. 

The element "assets" represents things owned; the element "capital" represents the ownership in these things.

The Equation Expressed in the Form of a Balance Sheet Continuing our illustration, let us assume that Mr. Carson immediately spent $800 for merchandise, $650 for store fixtures, and $50 for supplies. This left a cash balance of $500. 

It is evident that the business now has four assets amounting to $2000, which sum also represents Mr. Carson's proprietorship.

It is cumbersome to express the equality of these assets and the proprietorship in the form of an equation. Instead a statement may be drawn up known as a Balance Sheet, which will accomplish the same purpose. The assets are listed on the left-hand side, and the proprietorship on the right-hand side in this statement. The name Balance Sheet is appropriate because the two sets of facts relating to assets and capital are equal to or balance each other. Carson's assets and his capital are shown in the Balance Sheet on the next page.

CAPITAL 

9 Cash

ASSETS 

James Carson

BALANCE SHEET 

February 1, 19CAPITAL 

$500 J. Carson, Capital $2000

Merchandise Stock 

800 

Furniture and Fixtures 650 Supplies and Services 

50 $2000 $2000 

Summary 

1. The fundamental capital equation shows the equality between its two elements, assets and capital. 

2. The equation may be expressed in the form of a Balance Sheet. The total of the assets listed on the left-hand side equals the proprietor's capital listed on the right-hand side of the statement.

1. What are the elements of the fundamental capital equation? 

2. Distinguish between "assets" and "capital.' 

3. Why is the statement showing the equality between assets and capital called a Balance Sheet? 

4. What two terms are used in bookkeeping to signify ownership?

4. THE ACCOUNT The Need for Keeping Accounts 

It has already been pointed out that assets constantly undergo changes in value in the daily course of business. It would not be practical every time an asset changes in value to draw up a revised Balance Sheet. To record these changes readily, separate accounts may be set up for the various items that make up the Balance Sheet, so that for each asset there is an account in which the changes in the asset may be noted as they occur. Similarly an account may be set up for the capital. We shall see in the next lesson how changes are recorded in the accounts. In its simplest form the account resembles the letter T and is therefore referred to as the "T" account.

Example: On March 1, 19—, Henry Blake began business with an investment of $1000 cash and $2000 merchandise. 

His Balance Sheet on this date was: 

Henry Blake

BALANCE SHEET 

Cash

March 1, 19ASSETS CAPITAL 

$1000 H. Blake, Capital $3000 2000 $3000 $3000 

Merchandise Stock 

The items in the Balance Sheet may be set up in the following three accounts: 19March 1 

CASH 

$1000 11

19March 1 

MERCHANDISE STOCK 

$2000

H. BLAKE, CAPITAL 

19March 1 $3000 Each account has two sides, the left or Debit side and the right or Credit side. The values of the assets Cash and Merchandise Stock appear on the debit side in the accounts, corresponding to their left-hand position both in the equation and in the balance sheet. Likewise, the proprietor's capital is shown on the credit side of the account, corresponding to its´ right-hand position both in the equation and in the balance sheet. A record made on the debit or credit side of an account is known as an entry.

Three distinct methods of reporting assets and capital have thus far been shown: the fundamental equation, the balance sheet, and the accounts. In each method, assets appear on the left-hand or debit side, capital on the right-hand or credit side. This particular arrangement is purely arbitrary, but it is universally followed. 

Rules for Recording the Value of Assets and Capital ASSET ACCOUNTS 

Debit an asset account for the value of the asset it represents 

THE CAPITAL ACCOUNT 

Credit the capital account for the proprietor's interest in the business.

THE ACCOUNT 

13 

Summary 

1. The items on the Balance Sheet may be set up in separate accounts. 

2. The account is a bookkeeping device for recording the value of a Balance Sheet item. 

3. An asset account is debited for the value of the asset it represents. 

4. The capital account is credited for the proprietorship.

5. CHANGES IN ASSETS Equal Exchange of Assets 

PROBLEM: Thomas Lorraine began business August 1, 19with a cash investment of $2000. August 2, he bought a stock of merchandise for $900, paying cash. 

The fundamental capital equation expressing the condition at the opening of the business on August 1 is (a) Cash $2000 T. Lorraine, Capital $2000

What effect has the purchase of merchandise for cash on the two elements in the equation? The asset Cash is decreased by $900; hence, the owner's capital in that asset is decreased by $900. At the same time a new asset, Merchandise Stock, appears at a value of $900, and increases Mr. Lorraine's capital by $900. It is important to note that every change in assets produces a corresponding change in capital. These changes may now be shown in the fundamental equation. Hence (b) 

Cash $2000 - $900 

Merchandise Stock $900 Capital $2000-$900+$900 or (c) Cash $1100+Stock $900=T. Lorraine, Capital $2000 

Theoretically, capital has undergone two changes, a decrease by the reduction of cash and an increase by the acquisition of merchandise. This is shown in equation b. Practically, the total value of capital is not changed, since the total value of the assets remains the same through the equal exchange between cash and merchandise. Equation c, which is a condensed form of equation b, shows that the capital has remained the same in value.

The Account Records 

The records in the appropriate accounts for the two 15

transactions in the problem will now be considered. The accounts set up for the investment on August 1, corresponding to equation (a) on page 15 are

CASH 

19Aug. 1 $2000

T. LORRAINE, CAPITAL 

19Aug. 1 $2000 The records for the transaction of August 2 should show an increase of merchandise and a decrease of cash. The problem arising at this point is to show the record for the decrease in cash. This might be indicated as a direct subtraction on the debit side of the cash account, as follows:

CASH 

$2000 2 

900 19Aug. 1

This arrangement is unsatisfactory, because the record becomes confusing when several increases and decreases are recorded. The following example illustrates the difficulty of finding the cash on hand after several changes have been made. 19Aug. 1 12479 

CASH 

$2000 ++1 900 500 300 600 The proper method is to enter all increases in value of the asset on the side that shows the value of the asset at the beginning, and all decreases in the value of the asset on the opposite side of the account.

CHANGES IN ASSETS 

17 Thus, an increase in value of an asset is recorded on the debit side of the account and a decrease in value of an asset is recorded on the credit side of the account. Rearranging the cash record in the foregoing example, the Cash account reads 

CASH 

19Aug. 1 19147 $2000 Aug. 2 500 300 $900 600 This record shows clearly that a total of $2800 cash has been received and that a total of $1500 cash has been expended. Furthermore, the cash on hand can be readily found. 

Returning to our problem on page 15, the complete account records for the transactions of August 1 and 2 may now be set up. These are 

CASH 

19Aug. 1 19- $2000 Aug. 2 $900

MERCHANDISE STOCK

Rules for Recording Increases and Decreases in Assets

ASSET ACCOUNTS 

Debit an asset account for every Credit an asset account for every increase in value in the asset it rep- decrease in value in the asset it represents. resents.

Summary 

1. Four items of information may be obtained from an asset account: (a) the value of the asset when acquired, (b) increases in the value of the asset, (c) decreases in the value of the asset, (d) the net value of the asset after these changes have taken place. 

2. An asset account is debited to record an increase in value of the asset. 

3. An asset account is credited to record a decrease in value of the asset.

CHAPTER II  6. THE  The Meaning of Expense 

A merchant engages in business investing cash or other assets with the expectation of increasing his capital by deriving a profit out of the enterprise. He cannot accomplish this merely by buying merchandise and selling it above cost. 

He must rent space, advertise his wares, insure his stock and equipment, employ sales clerks and office help, and purchase many other kinds of supplies and services for operating his business efficiently. As these supplies and services are consumed, they decrease in value resulting in a corresponding decrease in capital. The term applied to this decrease in capital is expense. Therefore, expense may be defined as the decrease in capital resulting from the consumption of assets used in operating the business. 

Expense and profit are two opposing elements that account for the net change in capital. Each of these will be considered in turn in its relation to capital. 

PROBLEM: Paul Stedman went into business Feb. 1, 19with a cash investment of $4000. At the end of the first week his accounts stood as follows:

CASH MERCHANDISE STOCK 

1919- 19Feb. 1 $4000 Feb. 1 135 $120 40 

Feb. 5 $2500 2500 22 22

19Feb. 1 3 $120 40 

P. STEDMAN, CAPITAL 

19Feb. 1 $4000

Since no sales were made during the week, no profits were realized. The business paid $120 in advance for rent for one month and $40 for office books and stationery. Assuming that there was on hand $25 worth of the latter asset at the end of the week, we find that the asset rent has decreased $30 in value (for one week), and the asset office books and stationery has decreased $15 in value. These decreases in supplies and services have produced a corresponding expense of $45 or a decrease in Mr. Stedman's capital of $45.

Record of Changes in Capital 

How are changes in capital recorded? We have seen that the Capital account is credited for the proprietor's investment in the business. The principle observed for recording changes in the value of an asset is followed in recording changes in capital; namely, increases in capital are recorded on the same side as that on which the original record in the account appears and decreases are recorded on the opposite side. Since the original capital is recorded on the credit side of the Capital account, increases in capital are credited in this account and decreases in capital are debited in this account. 

The Profit and Loss Account 

If there were only a few changes in capital during a business period, it would be a simple matter to record such changes in the proprietor's Capital account. As there are actually very many changes in capital, they are recorded instead in temporary capital accounts. The use of temporary capital accounts helps to keep the main Capital account free from a large number of detailed records. In this way it be-

comes possible to show clearly in the proprietor's Capital account only the net change in capital from one period to another. These temporary capital accounts are branches of the main Capital account. 

Referring to our problem, we are now ready to make the records for the total decrease of $45 in the assets rent and stationery and the corresponding decrease in capital. The account Supplies and Services is credited with $30 for the decrease in rent and with $15 for the decrease in stationery. 

The corresponding decreases in capital or the expenses of $30 and $15 are shown as debits in a temporary capital account called Profit and Loss. This results in the following record:

PROFIT AND LOSS 19Feb. 6 $30 19Feb. 6 $30 15 15 It should be noted that while consumption of supplies and services and the corresponding reduction in capital goes on all the time, it is practical to record such changes periodically. 

Thus in our example, although the consumption of rent was continuous from February 1 to February 6 inclusive, the total decrease in value of the asset rent and in capital was not recorded until the close of the week. 

The complete record of Paul Stedman's accounts for the week may now be shown.

CASH MERCHANDISE STOCK 

19Feb. 1 19- $4000 Feb. 1 $120 19Feb. 5 $2500 35 40 2500

19Feb. 1 3 19- $120 Feb. 6 40 $30 15 

P. STEDMAN, CAPITAL 

19Feb. 1 $4000 

PROFIT AND LOSS 19Feb. 6 $30 15 The balance sheet drawn up from these accounts at the close of the week is 

Paul Stedman BALANCE SHEET 

February 6, 19ASSETS CAPITAL 

Cash

Merchandise Stock Supplies and Services Total Assets 

$1340 P. Stedman, Capital Feb. 1 $4000 

2500 Less Profit and Loss 115 $3955 Present Capital 45 $3955 

A comparison of Stedman's capital on Feb. 1, $4000, with his capital on Feb. 6, $3955, shows a decrease of $45, which corresponds to the total decrease of $45 in the asset supplies and services. 

Rule for Recording Expense 

PROFIT AND LOSS 

Debit the Profit and Loss account for decreases in capital resulting from the consumption of assets used in operating the business.

Summary 

1. Expense is a decrease in capital resulting from the consumption of supplies and services.

2. Changes in capital are recorded in a temporary capital account called Profit and Loss. 

3. Expense is recorded on the debit side of the Profit and Loss Account. 

4. The steady consumption of supplies and services and the corresponding decrease in capital are recorded at the end of the business period.

4. (a) What do the records on the debit side of the Profit and 

Loss account indicate? (b) Why are these records made in the Profit and Loss account rather than in the main Capital account?

7.  Case I-A Sale of the Entire Stock of Goods on Hand 

PROBLEM: E. Gray had $1000 cash and $500 worth of merchandise on hand Mar. 1, 19—. On Mar. 2, he disposed of his entire stock for $700 cash.

The fundamental equation reflecting Mr. Gray's financial condition on Mar. 1 is (a) 

Cash $1000) +Merchandise Stock $500) = E. Gray, Capital $1500

The sale on Mar. 2 affected the assets and the capital. 

Cash was increased by $700, resulting in an increase in capital of $700; merchandise stock was decreased by $500, resulting in a decrease in capital of $500. The revised equation, reflecting these modifications in assets and in capital, is (b) 

Cash $1000+$700| +Mdse. Stock $500-$500 which, when simplified, becomes (c) 

Capital $1500+$700-$500 Cash $1700 = E. Gray, Capital $1700 

Comparing equation a with equation c, it is evident that assets and capital have each had a net increase of $200. This increase is the result of the profit realized on the sale of $500worth of merchandise for $700. As explained in the discussion of the Profit and Loss account, increases and decreases in capital resulting from business operations are shown in temporary capital accounts. Changes in capital resulting from selling merchandise are recorded in a temporary capital account called the Sales Income account.

Two sets of account records result from the sale on Mar. 2. 

The first, showing the increases in cash and in capital, is 31

CASH 

19Mar. 2 $700 

SALES INCOME 

19Mar. 2 $700 The second set of records, showing the decreases in merchandise and in capital, is 

MERCHANDISE STOCK SALES INCOME 

1919- Mar. 2 $500 Mar. 2 $500 The complete business record for Mar. 1 and 2 is shown in the following group of accounts. It should be noted that the Sales Income account shows an increase in capital of $700 and a decrease in capital of $500, resulting in a net increase in capital or a profit of $200.

CASH 

19Mar. 1 2 $1000 700 

SALES. INCOME 

19Mar. 2 19- $500 Mar. 2 $700 

MERCHANDISE STOCK E. GRAY, CAPITAL 

19Mar. 1 19- $500 Mar. 2 $500 19Mar. 1 $1500

Rules for Recording Changes in Capital Resulting from Sales SALES INCOME Debit the Sales Income account Credit the Sales Income account for the decrease in capital resulting for the increase in capital resulting from the decrease of merchandise by from the increase in value of the assale. set received for a sale.

Summary 

1. The Sales Income account is a temporary capital account in which are recorded changes in capital resulting from sales. 

2. The increase in capital resulting from the receipt of cash for a sale is shown as a credit in the Sales Income account. 

3. The decrease in capital resulting from the decrease in merchandise is shown as a debit in the Sales Income account.

8.  (Continued) Case II-A Sale of Part of the Stock on Hand 

PROBLEM: George Thorpe's assets on Apr. 1, 19—, consisted of merchandise stock, $1500, and cash, $1300. During the week he made the following cash sales: Apr. 2, to Chas. 

Miles, $300; Apr. 4, to Fred Snyder, $250; Apr. 6, to Benj. 

Malter, $400. 

How did the sale of Apr. 2 affect assets and capital? Cash and capital were increased by $300. Merchandise and capital were decreased; the amount of this decrease, however, is not known, as there is no indication of the cost value of the goods sold. If this information were available, the record could be completed as in case I, by debiting Sales Income and crediting Merchandise Stock for the two decreases involved. In the absence of this information, the record for the sale may, temporarily, be indicated thus:

CASH 

19Apr. 2 $300 

MERCHANDISE STOCK 

19Apr. 2 $ ?

SALES INCOME 

19Apr. 2 19- $? Apr. 2 $300 It is not practicable every time a sale is made to ascertain and to record the cost of the goods sold. Hence, while the first part of the record, showing the increase in cash and in capital, can be made at the time of the sale, the second part of the record, showing the decrease in merchandise and in capital, must be postponed to some convenient time when stock is taken. The cost of sales made during the period may then be readily determined by deducting the cost of merchandise on hand from the total cost recorded in the Merchandise Stock account. 36

To illustrate, let us suppose that at the close of the first week in April, Mr. Thorpe took stock and found that the merchandise on hand, figured at cost, amounted to $900. His records, showing the cash and capital increases from the three sales, are

CASH 

19Apr. 2 246 $300 250 400 

SALES INCOME 

19Apr. 2 $300 4 250 400 Since the value of the stock on hand Apr. 1, as stated in the problem, was $1500, and the value of the stock on hand Apr. 6, as determined by taking stock, was $900, the difference of $600 represents the cost of the stock sold during the week. 

The total decrease in merchandise and in capital resulting from the three sales is recorded as follows:

MERCHANDISE STOCK SALES INCOME 

19Apr. 6 $600 19Apr. 6 $600 The complete account records for the problem are 

MERCHANDISE STOCK SALES INCOME 

1919- Apr. 1 $1500 Apr. 6 $600 19Apr. 6 19- $600 Apr. 2 $300 250 нео 400 

CASH 

19Apr. 1 $1300 2 300 250 400 

GEORGE THORPE, CAPITAL 

19Apr. 1 $2800

The Cash account shows a total of $2250, and the Merchandise Stock account a balance of $900. A comparison of these figures with the asset values on Apr. 1 shows that cash has increased $950, and that merchandise stock has decreased $600. This is equivalent to a net increase in assets of $350. 

The Sales Income account shows, on the credit side, a total increase in capital of $950, and on the debit side, a decrease in capital of $600. This is equivalent to a net increase in capital of $350, representing the profits realized on sales. 

The conclusions reached in these accounts may now be set forth in the balance sheet. 

George Thorpe

BALANCE SHEET 

April 6, 19 

ASSETS 

Cash 

Merchandise Stock Total Assets CAPITAL 

$2250 G. Thorpe, Capital Apr. 1 $2800 

900 Profit on Sales $3150 

Present Capital 

350 $3150 

Summary 

1. Generally, the cost of goods sold is not known until stock is taken and the cost of goods on hand is deducted from the total cost recorded in the Merchandise Stock account. 

2. An entry is made at the time of the sale debiting Cash and crediting Sales Income for the amount of cash received and the corresponding increase in capital. 

3. The entry for the decrease in merchandise and the corresponding decrease in capital is postponed until the cost of goods sold is determined.

(b) Which of these changes can be immediately recorded? Why? (c) Which of these changes generally cannot be immediately recorded? Why? (d) When may the changes not recorded at the time of sale be recorded? Why?

9. THE  

The chapter, thus far, has dealt with changes in capital resulting from operating the business. It has been shown that decreases in capital resulting from the consumption of supplies and services are recorded in the Profit and Loss account, and all capital changes resulting from the sale of merchandise are recorded in the Sales Income account. 

Temporary and Permanent Changes in Capital 

Changes in capital are also brought about by the proprietor's additions and withdrawals of assets. These changes may be either permanent or temporary in character. For example, the investment of additional cash, the turning over of a piece of equipment, as a desk, to the business are permanent additions in assets and in capital. The proprietor's withdrawal of cash with the fixed intention of operating the business with a reduced capital represents a permanent reduction in capital. On the other hand, additions and withdrawals are made from time to time not because the business is under or over-capitalized, but because some temporary benefit is to be derived either by the business or by the proprietor, personally. For example, the business may be going through a period of financial stress and the proprietor may advance a sum of money out of private funds as a temporary business loan. Common examples of withdrawals are drawings of cash by the proprietor for personal needs, taking merchandise for personal use, and paying personal debts with business funds. 

These withdrawals are made in expectation of profits which the proprietor hopes to realize. Withdrawals, consequently, are not reductions in the proprietor's original investment but rather drawings in advance of profits which he hopes will be realized by the end of the period. 

Permanent additions to and reductions in the proprietor's investment are recorded directly in the proprietor's Capital account. Temporary advances or withdrawals by the pro- 42

prietor are recorded in a special capital account, known as the 

Proprietor's Personal account. The titles Drawing Account and Private Account are also used. A few cases will illustrate the use of this account. 

1. On May 10 Claude Murray advanced out of private funds $500 cash to his business to tide over a dull period. 

The records for this loan are 

CASH

Rules for Debiting and Crediting Capital Accounts CAPITAL ACCOUNTS 

Debit a Capital account to record a decrease in capital.

Credit a Capital account to record an increase in capital.

Summary 

1. The Capital account is used to record the increase in capital when the proprietor makes an additional investment. 

2. The Capital account is used to record the decrease in

capital when the proprietor withdraws assets for the purpose of decreasing his investment. 

3. The Proprietor's Personal account is used to record the increase in capital when the proprietor makes a loan to the business. 

4. The Proprietor's Personal account is used to record the decrease in capital when the proprietor makes a withdrawal against future profits.

10.  The Purpose of Closing the Temporary Capital Accounts 

The special capital accounts, Profit and Loss, Sales Income, and the Proprietor's Personal account, have been presented to show their use in recording changes in capital arising from business operations. The point has been stressed that these capital accounts are only temporary in character, the object being to keep the original Capital account clear of the numerous records pertaining to income, loss, and the proprietor's personal dealings with the business. At the close of the business period, which is usually a year or six months, the temporary capital accounts are summarized and the net results, showing net increases or net decreases in capital, are carried into the original Capital account, so that the entire capital may be ascertained and shown on the books as the proprietor's investment for the next business period. The process of doing this is known as "closing the accounts." 

To illustrate the steps involved, the following temporary capital accounts will be used:

SALES INCOME 

1919- 

June 30 Cost of Sales 

$1230 June 8 23 28 ∞428 $ 240 14 428 547 465 

PROFIT AND LOSS 19June 30 Supplies and Services 

Expense 

30 Fixtures Expense 

$125 15 51

MILTON CROSS, PERSONAL 

19June 15 Cash 

26 Merchandise Stock 

$50 35 

Step I-Closing the Sales Income Account 

The Sales Income account sets forth two opposing sets of facts, the credits, representing the increases, and the debit, representing the decrease in capital resulting from sales. The difference between the total credits, $1680, and the debit, $1230, represents the net increase in capital of $450, the profit realized on sales. This profit may now be transferred to the 

Profit and Loss account, so that all items of profit and loss may be assembled in one account. This is accomplished by debiting Sales Income and crediting Profit and Loss for $450.

The transfer or closing out of the net sales profit from the Sales Income account to the Profit and Loss account is shown in the following accounts.

SALES INCOME 

1919- 

June 30 Cost of Sales 

30 To Profit and Loss $1230 June 8 450 $ 240 14 428 23 547 28 465 $1680 $1680 19PROFIT AND LOSS

June 30 Supplies and Services 

Expense 

30 Fixtures Expense 

19June 30 Sales Profit $450 $125 15 

Step II-Closing the Profit and Loss Account 

The two opposing sets of facts recorded in this account are the profits on the credit side amounting to $450, and the

losses on the debit side amounting to $140. The difference between the two represents the net profit of $310 resulting from operating the business. The Profit and Loss account may now be closed by transferring this net profit to the proprietor's Personal account.

The question may be raised at this point why the balance of the Profit and Loss account should be carried to the proprietor's Personal account rather than to his original Capital account. Two important considerations should be noted. 

First, profits are the reward to the proprietor for the investment of capital and the application of his personal services to the successful management of the business. Secondly, the proprietor's withdrawals are made, not with the intention of reducing the invested capital, but rather in anticipation of profits to be realized. It seems logical, therefore, to apply the profits earned over a business period against the withdrawals made by the proprietor during that time. 

The closing of the Profit and Loss account in the illustration is effected by debiting Profit and Loss and crediting Milton Cross, Personal for $310. This is indicated in the following accounts: 

19PROFIT AND LOSS 

19June 30 Sales Profit $450 $125 15 310 $450 $450 

June 30 Supplies and Services 

Expense 

30 Fixtures Expense 30 To M. Cross, Personal MILTON CROSS, PERSONAL 

1919- 

June 15 Cash 

26 Merchandise Stock 

$50 June 30 Net Profit 35 $310 

Step III-Closing the Proprietor's Personal Account 

The proprietor's Personal account shows at this stage an

increase in capital of $310 on the credit side resulting from the net profits realized, and a decrease in capital of $85 on the debit side resulting from withdrawals of cash and merchandise. The difference between the two, $225, is the net increase in capital for the month from all sources. 

It is entirely optional with the proprietor whether this balance in his Personal account is to remain in that account or is to be closed into his permanent Capital account. The first method is followed if he plans to draw against this balance. If, on the other hand, it is the proprietor's intention to increase his investment in the business by retaining his profits in the business, the balance in the Personal account should be transferred to the Capital account. The latter procedure is the one more generally followed and is here illustrated. The transfer is made by a debit to Milton Cross, Personal, and a credit to Milton Cross, Capital, of $225. 

MILTON CROSS, PERSONAL

Net Increase in Capital Treatment of Net Profit and Drawings on the Balance Sheet 

In closing the Profit and Loss account to the proprietor's Personal account, it was pointed out that drawings are made by the proprietor from time to time in anticipation of profits. 

The relationship of drawings to profits should be indicated on the balance sheet, as shown in the following model.

Cash

Summary 

1. At the close or the business period the temporary capital accounts are closed and the net increase or decrease resulting from the closing of these accounts is recorded in the permanent Capital account. 

2. The Sales Income account is closed by transferring the balance to the Profit and Loss account. A credit balance of the Sales Income account represents a profit and is transferred by debiting Sales Income and crediting Profit and

Loss. A debit balance of the Sales Income account represents a loss and is transferred by crediting Sales Income and debiting Profit and Loss. 

3. The Profit and Loss account is closed by transferring the balance to the proprietor's Personal account. A credit balance of the Profit and Loss account represents a net profit and is transferred by debiting Profit and Loss and crediting the proprietor's Personal account. A debit balance of the 

Profit and Loss account represents a net loss and is transferred by crediting Profit and Loss and debiting the proprietor's Personal account. 

4. The proprietor's Personal account is closed by transferring the balance to the proprietor's Capital account. A credit balance of the proprietor's Personal account represents a net increase in capital and is transferred by debiting the proprietor's Personal account and crediting the proprietor's Capital account. A debit balance of the proprietor's

Personal account represents a net decrease in capital and is transferred by crediting the proprietor's Personal account and debiting the proprietor's Capital account.

11. THE  Purpose of the Statement of Profit and Loss 

In the process of closing the temporary capital accounts all profit and loss items are summarized in the Profit and Loss account, leading to the conclusion that a net profit has been realized or a net loss sustained. In other words, the 

Profit and Loss account shows not only the net result of operating the business, but also all the elements of profit and loss that have contributed towards this result. At the time of closing the temporary capital accounts a Statement of Profit and Loss is prepared for conveying this information to the proprietor. Since the primary object in engaging in business is to realize a profit on one's investment, the efficient management of the business is judged very largely by the results accomplished and the factors that produced them. The object of the Statement of Profit and Loss is to present in nontechnical form the elements that have produced a net profit or loss over a business period.

To illustrate the statement of profit and loss, the following account records of Stephen Gale's business for the month of 

March, 19, will be considered.

CASH 

1919- 1919- Mar. 1 $860 Mar. 2 $80 Mar. 1 

MERCHANDISE STOCK 

$1640 Mar. 31 $1645 320 11 735 10 405 11 735 20 820 16 370 16 25 20 285 20 820 25 316 24 10 29 394

The statement of profit and loss contains two sections, the trading section and the operating expense section. The trading section in which the gross profit on sales is established presents in greater detail the facts contained in the completed Sales Income account. The operating expense section. shows the consumption and the wear and tear of assets used in operating the business. The difference between the results established in the two sections, namely, the "gross profit on sales" and the "total operating expense" represents the net profit or the net loss for the period. 

Relation of Statement of Profit and Loss to Balance Sheet 

Stephen Gale's net worth on March 1 as reported in his capital account was $3000. The net profit of $340 for the month brings his net worth on March 31 up to $3340. Referring to the accounts, the balance sheet at the end of the month is:

BALANCE SHEET 1. The balance sheet shows that capital has increased $340 from March 1 to March 31. 

STATEMENT OF PROFIT AND LOSS 

1. The statement of profit and loss shows how this increase of $340 was produced during the month.

ASSETS CAPITAL S.GALE'S BALANCE SHEET MARCH 1 

2. In this balance sheet the net increase in capital is determined by subtracting the proprietorship at the beginning of the period from the total value of the assets at the end of the period. 

3. The balance sheet is a picture of the financial condition of the business at a particular moment, in this case at the close of business on March 31. The statement is therefore dated March 31, 19-. 

2. In the statement of profit and loss the net profit is determined by finding the gross profit on sales and deducting the total operating expense. 

3. The statement of profit and loss covers the activities of the business over a certain period, in this case from

March 1 to March 31. The statement is therefore dated 

For the Month Ended March 

31, 19-. 

The net profit or the net loss may be obtained independently in each statement. It may be assumed that the statements are correct if they agree as to the net profit or the net loss.

INCREASE ACCOUNTED FOR IN

PROFIT ASSETS CAPITAL S.GALE'S BALANCE SHEET MARCH 31

Summary 

1. The purpose of the statement of profit and loss is to show in a simple nontechnical form the various items of information gathered from the accounts leading to the net profit or the net loss for the period. 

2. The balance sheet reflects the financial condition of the business at a particular time whereas the statement of profit and loss reflects the activities of the business during a given period.

CHAPTER III THE  13. THE COMPLETE ACCOUNT FORM 

The "T" form of account employed thus far is serviceable for drill exercises because of its simplicity. In actual bookkeeping practice, however, special columns are provided on both sides of the account for writing details that make the record complete. 

The following form shows the account ruling generally used. 

TITLE OF THE ACCOUNT Year mon. da.

Closing the Account 

Every account, it has been shown, records two opposing sets of facts relating to the asset or to the capital it represents. 

The totals in the two money columns show the total increases and the total decreases. If at any time the total debits equal the total credits, the account is said to be in balance and may be ruled off. When this is done, the account is closed. Models of a closed asset account and a closed capital account are shown in the following illustration.

Supplies and Services

Mar 3 Fuel 9 Repairs

Balancing the Account 

If the totals on the two sides of an account are unequal, the difference between the two represents the net value of the asset or the capital accounted for. This difference is referred to as the Balance of the account. Periodically, accounts having balances are ruled off and the balance is brought down as the initial record of the account for the next period. The process of doing this is known as balancing the account. The following

COMPLETE ACCOUNT FORM 

77 illustration shows the balancing of an asset and a capital account. Note the use of the small pencil figures for obtaining the footings, i.e., the money column totals, and the balance in the Cash account Cash

Summary 

1. In the complete account form each side contains columns for the date, explanation, and amount for each entry. 

2. When the total debits equal the total credits, the account is in balance. The account may be closed by entering these totals and ruling off the two sides as shown in the two accounts on page 76. 

3. When the total of one side is greater than the total of the other, the difference is known as the balance of the ac-

count. Balancing the account consists of entering the balance on the lesser side, closing the account and bringing the balance down on the greater side.

14.  

The accounts of a business, taken collectively, constitute a book of record known as the ledger. 

A model ledger, containing the account records for a month and the closing entries at the end of the period, is presented on pages 83, 84, and 85. The student is advised to make a study of this model, noting, in particular, the following features. 

1. The title of the account is written somewhat larger than the rest of the record. 

2. The month is written once only on either side of the account. 

3. The money columns in every account are footed in pencil preparatory to closing or balancing the account. 

4. The balance of an account is calculated in pencil in the explanation column on the side that has the larger total. 

5. The balance brought down in the proprietor's Capital account is called the Present Worth, which signifies his net worth or interest in the business at the close of the period. 

6. In ruling off an account when it is closed or balanced, a single line is drawn across both money columns, close below the last line used on the side that has the larger number of entries. Double lines are ruled underneath the totals, across the entire page except the explanation columns. The ruling on both sides of the account should be on the same level. The present tendency is to economize in time by ruling in black ink, although many bookkeepers still favor red ink. 

7. Entries marked * are balances to be brought down for the new period. Although they appear on the deduction side of the account, they do not represent decreases in assets or in capital; on the contrary, they indicate the net value of the asset or the capital at the time of balancing. For this reason, many 81

bookkeepers prefer to write the balance entry for equalizing the two sides of the account in red ink. 

8. In the Merchandise Stock and the Supplies and Services accounts, the term Inventory is used instead of Balance. 

The figures stated in these inventories are obtained not by the subtraction of one total from the other, but by a physical count, known as taking stock, of the property represented by the account. The quantities thus found are multiplied by the unit costs of the various items, and the individual amounts obtained are added, giving the inventory figure for the account. To illustrate, in the account. 

1919- 10 Oct. 1 Rent 3 Coal Insurance $ 90.00 

Oct. 5 Overcharge $10.00 65.00 26.00 181.00 the numerical difference of $171 between the two totals is not the inventory value of the supplies and services. The value of this asset is obtained by making an actual count of the supplies on hand and determining the value of paid services that have not yet been rendered. Assuming that this is done and an inventory figure of $45 is obtained, there is still an unaccounted difference of $126 ($171-$45). This account, apparently, represents the decrease in the asset Supplies and Services resulting from consumption. Consequently, the supplies and services expense can be determined only after the inventory value of the supplies and services has been determined.

In taking inventory, a schedule is prepared, listing each item, the quantity on hand, the cost price per unit, e.g., lb., doz., cwt., ea., etc., the total value of each item, and, finally, the total value of all the items. Stock sheets for inventorying the merchandise on hand are prepared in the same way. A model inventory sheet is shown in the following illustration.

Summary 

1. The accounts are kept collectively in one book called the ledger. 

2. The balance of an account at the end of a period indicates the value of the asset or capital it represents. 

3. Preparing an inventory of merchandise or supplies consists of (1) counting the number of articles on hand, (2) listing these articles with their quantities and unit cost on the inventory sheets, (3) finding the total value of each item, and (4) finding the total value of all the items. 

4. Forwarding an account consists of entering the totals of each column on the last line when the bottom of a ledger page is reached and repeating these totals at the top of the new page of the account. 

5. The accuracy of the work performed in closing and balancing the accounts can be tested by preparing a balance sheet from the balances brought down in the various accounts.

CHAPTER IV ASSET ACCOUNTS FOR THE EXTENSION OF CREDIT 15. ACCOUNTS RECEIVABLE Extension of Credit 

In buying and selling merchandise one of the important factors involved in every transaction is the element of payment, commonly referred to as the terms of purchase or the terms of sale. 

We shall consider first the case of a purchase. An article that you wish to purchase is offered at $100 by each of two firms, A and B. A's terms of payment are spot cash. B's terms are 

30 days' time; that is, B is willing to wait 30 days for the payment of the article. If you purchase from A you must have $100 cash in hand with which to pay. If you purchase from B you require no immediate funds and have 30 days in which to raise the cash due B. If you sell the article above cost within the 30 days you can pay off your indebtedness to B and realize a profit without any initial investment in purchasing the article. If all your purchases had to be made for cash you would be limited in the quantity of goods you could obtain by the amount of ready cash you had to give in payment. The extension of the time of payment enables you to raise the funds due as you sell your goods, and thus increases your purchasing capacity. 

Let us now consider the case of a sale. If you offered the article in the foregoing illustration for sale at $125, terms cash, a buyer would have to have the ready funds with which to pay. 

If, on the other hand, you offered to sell on 20 days' time, you could attract customers who did not have immediately available funds but who could by resale raise the sum due you. 

In short, buying on time enables you to acquire a larger stock and selling on time creates a readier market for your goods. 

Buying and selling on time payment is known as trading on 90

ACCOUNTS RECEIVABLE 

91 credit. By a system of credit is meant an arrangement whereby commodities change hands on the assurance of the buyer that he will pay at the expiration of a definite time. This element in trading gives rise to a new type of business transaction, the bookkeeping phase of which will now be considered. 

On Oct. 1 the following sales were made: (1) 5 bales Cotton to H. Langer at $60 each for cash. (2) 5 bales Cotton to G. Daly at $62 each on 30 days' credit.

Analysis of the Sales 

The first sale involved a receipt of cash of $300, a decrease of merchandise amounting to the cost of 5 bales of cotton, and an increase in capital represented by the profit made. In the second sale in place of cash, a claim against G. Daly for $310 was acquired through his promise to pay, either expressed verbally or implied in his acceptance of the merchandise subject to the terms imposed. This claim against Daly is enforceable at law and constitutes one of the assets of the business. Thus, our original conception of assets as things owned must now be extended to include money claims that the business holds against others. 

The asset name given to money claims arising from sales on account, that is, on the buyer's verbal or implied promise to pay, is Accounts Receivable. Records of Accounts Receivable are kept in individual personal accounts with customers. Those against whom these claims are held are known as Debtors to the business.

The account records for the two sales are 19Oct. 1 

CASH 

$300 19Oct. 1 

G. DALY 

$310

ASSET ACCOUNTS FOR EXTENSION OF CREDIT SALES INCOME 

19Oct. 1 1 $300 310 The term charge is frequently used in place of debit. Thus, we may say that G. Daly's account has been charged with $310, that G. Daly's account is a charge account, and that 

G. Daly is a charge customer.

Recording Returns and Payments from Customers 

(1) On Oct. 3, G. Daly returned 1 bale of cotton sold him on Oct. 1 and received credit for $62. As a result of this return the claim against G. Daly is reduced by $62. Therefore, G. Daly's account must be credited with $62 to show the de crease in the asset Accounts Receivable. Corresponding to this decrease there is a decrease in capital which is shown as a debit of $62 in the Sales Income account. The record at this time for the sales return is 

G. DALY SALES INCOME 

19Oct. 3 Return $62 

Oct. 3 Return $62 19No record is made of the increase in merchandise and the corresponding increase in capital resulting from this return, since no record was made of the decrease in merchandise and in capital at the time of the sale. (2) On Oct. 31, G. Daly's check for $248 was received in payment of the balance of his account. This results in an increase in the asset Cash and a decrease in the asset Accounts Receivable. G. Daly's account should now be in balance. The following is the complete record made for the sale, the return, and the cash settlement.

Treatment of Accounts Receivable on the Balance Sheet 

The various claims against customers are listed as assets: on the balance sheet under the head of "Accounts Receivable." When, however, the number of such accounts is large, it is more convenient to show only the total indebtedness. 

In such cases the item appears on the balance sheet as "Accounts Receivable $.... "The following balance sheet shows how the Accounts Receivable may be listed when there are only a few customers.

Summary

2. A sale on credit results in a claim against a customer. 

This claim is an asset and is known as an Account Receivable.

3. A separate account is kept with each charge customer. 

The claims against the customer are shown on the debit side; decreases in these claims are shown on the credit side. 

4. The claims of customers may be shown collectively or listed individually as assets on the Balance Sheet under the head of Accounts Receivable.

16. INTRODUCTION OF BUSINESS PAPERS Sources of Information for the Bookkeeping Records 

We have ascertained the purpose of keeping books and studied the account form of record necessary to achieve this purpose. We shall now consider the sources of information for the bookkeeping records. The bookkeeper must have some means of obtaining reliable information concerning every business act, so that his bookkeeping records may be accurate and complete. Thus, the bookkeeper's problem is twofold: (1) to obtain the data relating to the transactions engaged in, and (2) to record these facts in the proper accounts, so that statements may be drawn up periodically to show the progress made and the financial condition of the business. 

Bookkeeping records cannot be made from hearsay. In general, business transactions are evidenced by some form of writing. For example, a purchase or a sale of goods is accompanied by an invoice, a payment of rent by a rent receipt, a payment of wages by a payroll sheet or a time ticket, a payment for services by a bill, such as a telephone bill, or an electric current bill. Business papers are the sources from which the bookkeeping records are made. 

Incoming and Outgoing Business Papers 

Business papers are of two kinds: (1) Incoming papers received by the firm from outside sources, such as purchase invoices, service bills, sales orders, checks and notes from customers; (2) Outgoing papers issued by the firm, such as purchase orders, sales invoices, checks and notes given by the firm. All these papers are used by the bookkeeper to prepare his records. In a small office the bookkeeper prepares the outgoing papers and keeps a file of the incoming papers. In large houses this work is assigned to clerks. 

The student must be expert in the use of the common business forms. In this text these papers are introduced in connection with the bookkeeping records to which they give rise, so that the relationship between the two may be constantly 101

ASSET ACCOUNTS FOR EXTENSION OF CREDIT 

kept in mind. Having considered the bookkeeping records for accounts receivable, we shall now direct our attention to the business forms commonly employed in dealings between a firm and its charge customers. These are (1) the sales order, (2) the sales invoice, (3) the check, and (4) the statement of account. Model forms with comments are presented in the pages following.

THE SALES ORDER No. 1741

The order is then submitted to the credit department. If the customer is in good standing, the order is approved and passed on to the stock department to be filled. 

When the goods are ready for shipment, one copy of the order is sent to the office, where the prices are entered and the sales invoice is prepared. The other copy is kept on file in the shipping department. Sometimes a third copy is used as a shipping memorandum that is enclosed with the goods. In some houses the shipment is made up from an independent shipping memorandum, which is made out in the shipping department after the order has been approved.

Remittances from customers are, in the main, made in the form of checks. The writing of this form involves (1) filling out the check stub, and (2) writing the check. The stub furnishes the customer with a record of the payment after the check is issued. It is a good rule always to write the stub record first. 

This precludes the possibility of sending off a check without having a record of the payment.

ASSET ACCOUNTS FOR EXTENSION OF CREDIT 

Great care must be exercised in writing checks. The following precautions should be observed. 

1. All blank spaces should be filled in. 

2. The name of the payee (the one to whom the check is made payable) should be written exactly as he spells it. 

3. There should be no space between the dollar sign and the first digit in the amount, and, likewise, none between the last digit and the fraction expressing cents. Thus, the amount should be written $32545400, not $ 325 45/100. 

4. In spelling out the amount, there should be no margin at the beginning of the line. The fraction for cents should be written close up to the last word, and a line drawn through the unused portion of the space. 

5. There must be no erasure or alteration, as either will make the check void.

BUSINESS PAPERS 

105 Periodically, usually on the first of every month, a statement of account is sent to each charge customer whose account is open. This is done to furnish the debtor with a memorandum that he can compare with his record for the previous month. The statement serves also as a reminder of payment of any amounts that are past due. 

The monthly statement of account is prepared from the customer's account and shows: (1) the balance at the beginning of the month, (2) the charges entered during the month, (3) the items credited in the account during the month, and (4) the balance due at the end of the month. 

Summary 

1. Business papers are the source of information from which the bookkeeping records are prepared. 

2. Business papers are of two kinds, incoming and outgoing. 

3. The sales order is used for preparing a shipment of goods to a customer. 

4. The sales invoice is prepared from the sales order. 

The original copy is sent to the customer, and the duplicate copy is retained for office use in preparing the bookkeeping record of the sale. 

5. The check is the most common means employed in business for making payments. The stub records of outgoing checks are used for making the bookkeeping record of payments by check. 

6. At the beginning of each month a statement of account is sent to each charge customer whose account is open. 

1. Daniels received an order for knit goods from Hamilton by telephone. The order was confirmed the next day in writing. The goods were immediately shipped. Two weeks later Daniels received. a check in part payment. At the end of the month Hamilton discovered that Daniels had charged his account with an additional sale.

18. NOTES RECEIVABLE The Promissory Note as an Instrument of Credit 

In a sale on account, the seller relies on the buyer's promise, either verbal or implied, to pay. Promises of payment may be made also in writing. An instrument containing a promise to pay is called a Promissory Note. A sale on account and a sale on a promissory note are both credit transactions; in each case the seller relies on the buyer's integrity and ability to meet his indebtedness at some future time. However, firms sometimes sell on notes where they might hesitate to extend credit on account. There are several reasons for this discrimination. 

In the first place, the note fixes definitely the date of maturity, and thus imposes upon the maker of the note the obligation of being prompt in his payment. A note issued, for example, Jan. 5, payable in 30 days, falls due on Feb. 4 and is presented to the maker on that day. He is required to pay in full; he has no choice of postponing payment or making a part payment, either of which he might be tempted to do in the case of a sale on account.

Secondly, the written promise is considered evidence of the maker's liability. He practically acknowledges his indebtedness, whereas in the case of an account receivable the customer might dispute the sum involved or the terms imposed. 

Thirdly, the promissory note is useful as an instrument of exchange. The words "to the order of" preceding the payee's name signify that payment is promised to the payee or to any party to whom the payee may transfer the note. The note is said to be negotiable, that is, it may be passed from hand to hand. The holder may convert the note into cash before maturity by discounting it at his bank, or he may offer it to a creditor in payment of purchases. Thus, he may use the note to good advantage without waiting for it to mature. 

A promissory note may be defined as an unconditional promise made in writing by one party to pay to the order of 110

NOTES RECEIVABLE 

111 

another party a specified sum of money on demand or at a fixed or determinable future time. In the promissory note illustrated below the time of payment is fixed or determinable by the statement "Two months after date (Oct. 5), making the note fall due on December 5. A note reading "On demand I promise to pay " is a demand note and must be paid by the maker of the note whenever it is presented to him for payment. If no time is expressed the note is payable on demand. 

PROMISSORY NOTE

ASSET ACCOUNTS FOR EXTENSION OF CREDIT Parties and Indorsements 

There are two primary parties to a promissory note, (1) the Maker, who makes the promise to pay, and (2) the Payee, the one to whom the promise is made. To negotiate the note to a third party, the payee must write his signature on the back of the instrument. The same is true in the negotiation of checks. This is known as indorsing the instrument. The one who indorses is called the Indorser: the one to whom the instrument is indorsed is called the Indorsee. 

There are several types of indorsements. Only the two most commonly used are presented here the indorsement in blank and the indorsement in full. 

INDORSEMENT IN BLANK INDORSEMENT IN FULL 

Stephen A. Perry Pay to the order of 

Samuel Withers 

Stephen A.Perry

The indorsement in blank contains only the payee's signature. This makes the instrument payable to bearer, i.e., to anyone holding it. The instrument is referred to as a "bearer" instrument. In the indorsement in full the payee directs payment to the order of a designated third party and then writes his signature. The advantage of the indorsement in full over that in blank is that it restricts the payment of the instrument to the party designated or his order. This feature becomes of first importance where the instrument has been lost or stolen. 

For this reason the prudent person uses the full indorsement in preference to the other.

Note Transactions with Customers 

Like accounts receivable, the promissory notes that a business acquires constitute one of its classes of assets. They are recorded

NOTES RECEIVABLE 

113 in one account under the general title Notes Receivable. This differs from the method used in recording accounts receivable in individual customers' accounts. No confusion results from treating all the notes in one account as there would with the accounts receivable, because each note is a complete record in itself.

1. A sale on credit may be on account or on a promissory note. 

2. The advantages of a sale on a note over a sale on account are: (a) the note fixes definitely the due date of the

ASSET ACCOUNTS FOR EXTENSION OF CREDIT 

debt, (b) it is an acknowledgment by the customer of the amount of his indebtedness, (c) it may be used as an instrument of exchange. 

3. The primary parties to a promissory note are (a) the maker, (b) the payee. 

4. The expression, "to the order of" makes the note negotiable. Before a note can be negotiated it must be indorsed. Indorsements are commonly made in blank or in full. 

5. A note may be received from a customer at the time the sale is made or at a later time. In the first case the entry is: Notes Receivable to Sales Income. In the second case there are two entries: (1) Accounts Receivable to Sales Income, and (2) Notes Receivable to Accounts Receivable.

CHAPTER V LIABILITIES 20. LIABILITIES AND THE FUNDAMENTAL EQUATION Liabilities Arising from the Extension of Credit 

In an earlier discussion it was shown that business expansion is controlled very largely by the system of credit. In its relation to any business enterprise this economic factor presents two phases (1) credit extended by the business to its customers, and (2) credit extended to the business by those from whom it makes its purchases. This chapter will be devoted to a consideration of the latter phase as it relates to the technique of bookkeeping. 

When credit is extended to customers, the business acquires assets in the form of claims designated as Accounts 

Receivable and Notes Receivable. When the business buys on credit, it contracts an obligation to pay at some future time. This creates a state of indebtedness, which is designated as a liability of the business. The one to whom the business is indebted is called a creditor of the business. So that, from the standpoint of the business, a liability may be defined as a debt that the business owes to a creditor. From the point of view of the creditor, a liability may be defined as a claim that he holds against the business. 

Relation of Liabilities to the Fundamental Equation 

In the initial fundamental equation Assets Capital, two related ideas are presented, the things owned and the interest or property right in these things. As long as the business is free from debt, the proprietor has the sole interest in the assets. As soon as the business incurs a liability, a claim against the business is created in favor of the creditor. This claim reduces the proprietor's net interest in the business. 

The following equation expresses on the one hand the total 124

LIABILITIES AND THE FUNDAMENTAL EQUATION 125 

value of the assets of the business and on the other hand the claims of the creditors plus the remaining interest of the proprietor in the business. 

Assets Liabilities + Capital 

Thus, there are now three elements in the fundamental equation—(1) assets, the things owned, (2) liabilities, the claims of the creditors against the assets, and (3) capital, the remaining interest of the proprietor in the assets. 

The student should not confuse the notion of creditors' claims against the assets with the idea of legal ownership of these assets. A merchant, for example, who has sundry assets amounting to $5000, buys merchandise on credit for $2000. The business now has assets amounting to $7000, a liability of $2000, and a capital of $5000. The owner has full title to the $7000 worth of assets and exercises absolute control over them. The creditor who sold him the merchandise surrendered his title or ownership in the goods at the time of sale, and acquired in its place a claim against the merchant's assets. This claim entitles him to payment of the debt when the term of credit expires, or, failing to receive such payment, enables him to enforce his claim through due process of law.

Changes of Liabilities in the Fundamental Equation 

(1) On Sept 1, 19, C. Green had on hand cash $1500, furniture and fixtures $500, and merchandise $2000. His business condition is expressed in the fundamental equation 

Cash $1500+ Fixtures $500 +Merchandise Stock $2000 or =C. Green, Capital $4000

Assets $4000 = Capital $4000 (2) On Sept. 2, he purchased merchandise of J. Flynn for $300 on account. The equation is changed to $500 

Cash $1500+ Fixtures $500 +Merchandise Stock $2300 or (J. Flynn's Claim $300 Assets $4300 +C. Green, Capital $4000

Liability $300 +Capital $4000

LIABILITIES 

The purchase on account has resulted in an increase of $300 in the asset Merchandise and an increase of $300 in the liability J. Flynn's Claim. No change has taken place in capital. (3) On Sept. 15, he paid J. Flynn $200 cash on account.

The equation now becomes 

Cash $1300+ Fixtures $500) +Merchandise Stock $2300 

J. Flynn's Claim $100 +C. Green, Capital $4000 or Assets $4100 = Liability $100+Capital $4000 The cash payment has produced a decrease of $200 in the asset Cash and a decrease of $200 in the liability J. Flynn's claim. Again, capital has not been affected. 

Liabilities on the Balance Sheet 

The balance sheet, it has been pointed out, shows in statement form the elements comprising the fundamental equation. The liabilities are reported with the capital on the right-hand side of the balance sheet. This arrangement presents the assets on one side and the claims of the creditors as well as the proprietor's interest on the other. The liabilities precede the capital because the owner's net worth is the interest remaining in the assets after the liabilities have been met. A model balance sheet is shown below. 

Murray Parker

1. When the business buys on credit, a liability is incurred. 

2. The person to whom the business is indebted for such a liability is called a creditor. 

3. The complete fundamental equation is: 

Assets Liabilities + Capital. 

4. Liabilities appear on the right-hand side of the balance sheet because they represent claims against the assets of the business.

21. ACCOUNTS PAYABLE Recording Transactions with Creditors 

When a purchase on credit is made, the promise to pay may, as in a sale, be expressed verbally or it may be implied in the terms of purchase, as "30 days' credit," "on account," etc.

In either case a liability arises which is technically known as an 

Account Payable. Records of Accounts Payable are kept in individual personal accounts with the creditors. A few typical transactions involving Accounts Payable will be considered. (1) Dec. 1, 19—, bought merchandise on account of Grady & Powell for $650. The purchase entry is

MERCHANDISE STOCK 

19Dec. 1 $650 

GRADY & POWELL 

19Dec. 1 $650 The increase in the asset Merchandise results in a debit to the account Merchandise Stock. The increase in the liability 

Accounts Payable results in a credit to the account Grady & Powell. The position of the liability record in the account corresponds to its position both in the fundamental equation and in the balance sheet. In all three forms, the liability appears on the right-hand or credit side. (2) Dec. 15, paid Grady & Powell $200 cash on account. 

The payment entry is

GRADY & POWELL 

19Dec. 15 $200 

CASH 

19 

Dec. 15 $200 The decrease in the liability Accounts Payable results in a debit to the account Grady & Powell. The decrease in the asset Cash is noted by a credit to the Cash account. Decreases 130

ACCOUNTS PAYABLE 

131 are recorded on the debit side of liability accounts in accordance with the principle of showing decreases on the side of the account opposite to that on which increases appear. (3) Dec. 20, returned to Grady & Powell imperfect merchandise which had been billed at $15. The entry for the return is 

GRADY & POWELL 

19Dec. 20 $15 

MERCHANDISE STOCK 

19Dec. 20 $15 The liability Accounts Payable is decreased, and the asset 

Merchandise is decreased; hence the entry — "Grady & Powell to Merchandise Stock, $15." At this point, the account with 

Grady & Powell shows an original credit of $650 and two offsetting debits amounting to $215. The difference of $435 is the amount still due. If this indebtedness at the time of closing the books has not been paid, the account is ruled off and the balance is brought down for the next period. The balancing of the account is illustrated in the following model. Note the additional data in the heading relating to the maximum amount of credit extended and the terms of payment. 

GRADY & POWELL 

610 Michigan Ave. 

Chicago, Ill. 

Credit Limit $1000

Terms 30 Days

Mdse. 650 00 31 23 20 

Ret'd Mdse.

Rules for Debiting and Crediting Accounts Payable CREDITOR 

Debit the personal account of the creditor when the liability Accounts Payable is decreased.

Credit the personal account of the creditor when the liability Accounts Payable is increased.

LIABILITIES The Purchase Order BUSINESS PAPERS 

Retailers and small jobbers order their goods verbally or by letter. Usually orders given verbally are later confirmed in writing. In well organized concerns a printed form called a Purchase 

Order is used. In large business organizations, where there are several departments, all the purchasing is done through a central agency known as the Purchasing Division. A request in writing, known as a Purchase Requisition, is made by the department requiring the article to be bought, and is submitted to the purchasing division. Here a purchase order is made out in duplicate. 

The original copy is sent to the concern from whom the purchase is to be made. The carbon copy is kept on file, pending the receipt of the invoice and the arrival of the goods. Purchase orders vary in arrangement and in content, each business adapting the form to its particular needs. In general, however, it contains the information given in the model illustrated here. 

PURCHASE ORDER 

No. 1489 

Refer to this number in your invoice

Trent Electrical Contracting Company 

358 Chestnut Street 

Boston, Mass.

To John P. Nugent Mfg. Co. 

124 Penn Ave. 

Pittsburgh, Pa. 

Please fill the following order:

Description
