Shipping Instructions 

American Rlwy. Express Price 

Dumont Starting Boxes, model 794 

each $16.75

Trent Electrical Contracting Company Per Gorge L. Matthews

ACCOUNTS PAYABLE 

133 

The Purchase Invoice 

The invoice, usually, is sent by mail by the seller and reaches the buyer before the goods arrive. On receipt of the latter, the invoice is checked as to quantities by the receiving clerk. 

It is then turned over to the order clerk, who compares it with the purchase order with reference to the items sent, the prices charged, and the terms stated. Finally, it is passed on to the accounting department, where the extensions, i.e. the quantity multiplied by the price, and the total are verified. A practical device for indicating the checking on the invoice by the respective parties concerned consists of a form that is stamped upon the invoice when it is received. In it are listed all the items to be checked and countersigned by those making the verifications. 

FORM FOR VERIFYING INVOICE 

Date received

No. Packages 

Quantities Prices 

Items sent. 

Mar. 20,19- ;base 

1. Benson

820

F. Barr

Date paid fs. 94 J. Ryan

Приле 

Check No. 

784 

Cash journal fs, 83 J. Ryan

The invoice is now ready to be entered on the books. Follow- ing this, it is filed in an Unpaid Purchase Invoice file. When it is due for payment, it is removed from this file, the payment records are noted on it in the space provided in the verification record, and the invoice is filed in the Paid Invoice file. 

The complete business routine involved in making a purchase on account in a highly departmentalized business is shown in the diagram on the next page.

LIABILITIES BUSINESS ROUTINE OF A PURCHASE 

1. Requisition for the goods 

2. Purchase order in duplicate 

3. Receipt of Invoice 

4. Invoice checked with goods received 

5. Invoice checked with purchase order 

6. Extensions, total and terms verified 

7. Bookkeeping record made of purchase. 

8. Invoice placed in Unpaid file 

9. Check issued for invoice 

10. Payment recorded on invoice 

11. Bookkeeping record made of payment 12. 

Invoice placed in Paid file

23. NOTES PAYABLE Purchasing on Notes 

Purchases on credit, like sales on credit, may be made on the buyer's verbal promise to pay or on his promissory note.

In the case of a sale on a note, the seller, we have seen, receives a note receivable. Looking at the transaction from the standpoint of the buyer, the note he issues is his written acknowledgment of indebtedness to the seller. The title 

Notes Payable expresses the liability on a note. Every note is a note payable on the books of the maker and a note receivable on the books of the payee. This is illustrated in the following diagram. 

SALE SELLER NOTE RECEIVABLE PURCHASE BUYER NOTE PAYABLE Note Transactions with Creditors 

1. Bought merchandise of Wilcox & Co., Aug. 5, 19—, for $800, giving them our 30-day note. The account records are: 

MERCHANDISE STOCK 

19Aug. 5 $800

NOTES PAYABLE 

19Aug. 5 $800

The asset Merchandise is increased, resulting in a debit to the Merchandise Stock account. A liability, Notes Payable, is created and, in accordance with the principle of showing 144

NOTES PAYABLE 

145 liabilities on the credit side of the account, the Notes Payable account is credited with $800. 

2. Paid Wilcox & Co. cash on Sept. 4, 19-, for our note due today. The account records for the payment of the note are: 

NOTES PAYABLE 

19Sept. 4 

$800 

CASH 

19Sept. 4 $800

The Notes Payable account is debited because the liability on the note has been wiped out. The credit to Cash represents a decrease in the asset Cash. The complete record in the note account at this point is: 19Sept. 4

NOTES PAYABLE 

19- $800 Aug. 5 30 da. $800 3. (a) Sept. 20, 19—, Bought of O. Cadman merchandise on account for $500. (b) Oct. 3, 19-—, Gave him our 30-day note for $350 to apply on account.

The entries for the two transactions are: 

MERCHANDISE STOCK 

19Sept. 20 $500 

O. CADMAN 

19Oct. 3 19- $350 Sept. 20 $500

NOTES PAYABLE 

19Oct. 3 30 da. $350

LIABILITIES 

(a) The Merchandise Stock account is debited for the increase in the asset Merchandise, and O. Cadman's account is credited for the increase in the liability Accounts Payable. (b) O. Cadman's account is debited for the decrease in the liability Accounts Payable, and the Notes Payable account is credited for the increase in the liability Notes Payable.

All the notes issued by the business are recorded in one account, Notes Payable. This differs from the method of recording the accounts payable in individual accounts with creditors. In a purchase on account the liability binds the buyer to the seller. In a purchase on a note the liability binds the buyer to the seller only as long as the seller retains the note. Should he indorse it to a third party, the creditor surrenders his claim and the buyer becomes liable to the holder of the note. For this reason notes payable are not recorded in individual accounts with creditors. The maker's detailed record of the note is contained on the stub which is filled out before the note is issued.

Rules for Debiting and Crediting Notes Payable 

These are similar to the rules governing Accounts Payable.

NOTES PAYABLE Debit the Notes Payable account Credit the Notes Payable account when the liability Notes Payable is de- when the liability Notes Payable is creased. increased.

We have thus far observed how assets, liabilities, and capital are affected by business transactions and have noted the account records for indicating the increases and decreases in value of these three elements. Bookkeeping may, therefore, be defined as the art of keeping accounts to show the changes in value of assets, liabilities, and capital resulting from business transactions.

Summary 

1. A purchase on credit may be on account or on a promissory note. 

2. The liability arising from the issuing of a note is recorded in the Notes Payable account. 

3. A note may be issued to a creditor when a purchase is made or at a later time. In the first case the entry is: 

Merchandise Stock to Notes Payable. In the second case there are two entries: (1) Merchandise Stock to Accounts Payable, and (2) Accounts Payable to Notes Payable.

CHAPTER VI  AND THE FINANCIAL STATEMENTS 25. THE TRIAL BALANCE The Principle of Double Entry 

Every business transaction produces changes in one or more of the three elements-assets, liabilities and capital-of the fundamental equation. These changes give rise to debit and credit records of equal amount in the ledger. The complete ledger record of a transaction therefore consists of a double entry, in which one account, at least, is debited and one account, at least, is credited. The following diagram illustrates these changes and the double entry records resulting from them. 

RESULTING DOUBLE ENTRIES ASSET (B)

NOTE: Any of the changes enumerated in column A may be accompanied by changes in more than one of three elements of the fundamental equation. For example, in a purchase in which there is a part payment in cash and a note for the balance, the increase of the asset Merchandise results in a decrease in the asset Cash and in an increase in the liability Notes Payable.

The Trial Balance of Totals 

Since in every double entry made the debit record equals in amount the credit record, it follows that the total of all the debit amounts in the ledger should, at any time, equal the total of all the credit amounts. The principle of double entry enables the bookkeeper to test the accuracy of his ledger work. The procedure is to foot in pencil all the money columns in all the accounts and to draw up a summary of these totals with their account titles. If no error has been made, a balance will be struck between the total debits and the total credits. This device is known as taking a Trial Balance.

Bookkeepers generally take a trial balance at the end of each month.

The trial balance in the model shown here is a summary of Arthur Grant's ledger as it appears at the end of January,

AND FINANCIAL STATEMENTS

Following the space for the account titles are the money columns for the account totals, the left-hand one for the debits and the right-hand one for the credits.

The Trial Balance of Balances 

If in the foregoing trial balance the cash balance of $2663.72 were entered instead of the total receipts and the total payments, the total of each money column would be diminished by $4864.20 and the equality between the two columns would not be disturbed. 

Similarly, if the balances of the remaining accounts were substituted for their debit and credit totals the equilibrium of the trial balance would be preserved. In practice, trial balances are usually made up of the balances of the accounts instead of their debit and credit totals. This type of trial balance will be used in this text. Arthur Grant's trial balance of balances is shown on the next page. The account with Howard Warner is omitted because it is in balance.

Limitations of the Trial Balance as a Test of Accuracy 

Although the purpose of the trial balance is to determine the correctness of the ledger, there are certain types of error that do not prevent the striking of a balance. The following types are instances in point: 

1. An incorrect amount recorded both in the debit and in the credit accounts involved. 

2. An entry in a wrong account, as a debit to Merchandise Stock instead of Sales Income for a return of goods from a customer. 

3. The complete omission of an entry for a transaction. 

On the other hand, errors like the following throw the ledger out of balance and prevent the striking of a balance in the trial balance. 

1. Omission of the debit or the credit entry for a transaction. 

2. Entering an amount on the wrong side of an account. 

3. Using two different amounts for the debit and the credit records of a double entry.

AND FINANCIAL STATEMENTS 

4. Incorrect addition or subtraction in an account. 

Although the ledger may be in balance, the footings of the trial balance will not be equal if any of the following errors have been committed in the trial balance itself. 

1. Incorrect copying of an amount in the trial balance. 

2. Entering an amount in the wrong column in the trial balance. 

3. Incorrect addition in the trial balance. 

4. Omission of an open account in the trial balance. 

It is evident from this analysis of types of error that the trial balance does not furnish conclusive proof of the correctness of the ledger. At best, the striking of a balance in the trial balance indicates that the work is probably correct. Failure to strike a balance is, of course, unmistakable evidence of error. The procedure for detecting such error is given in detail in a later chapter. In the meantime, the student, when encountering difficulty with his trial balance, should check back carefully his entries and calculations in the accounts and the transfer of the balances to the trial balance. The habit of constantly checking and testing in bookkeeping is the best safeguard against error.

Summary 

1. The double entry system of bookkeeping involves the recording of one or more debits and one or more credits for each transaction. The total amount of the debits for any one transaction must equal the total amount of the credits. 

2. Because of the equality between debits and credits for each transaction, the ledger should constantly be in balance. 

This condition is tested by taking a trial balance. 

3. A trial balance of totals is a list of the accounts in the ledger with their debit and credit footings. 

4. A trial balance of balances is a list of the accounts in the ledger with their respective debit or credit balances.

5. The striking of a trial balance when the trial balance is taken is reasonable though not conclusive proof of the accuracy of the ledger.

1. What is meant by the system of double entry bookkeeping?

26. CONSTRUCTION OF STATEMENTS FROM  

In general practice the trial balance is taken at the end of every month. As soon as a balance is struck the monthly statements of accounts with customers are prepared and sent out. 

This is followed by checking against the accounts payable balances the monthly statements of account received from creditors. 

The bookkeeper then proceeds with the bookkeeping records for the next month. This routine is followed month after month until the end of the business period, usually a year or a halfyear, is reached. At this time stock is taken and appraisals are made of the equipment on hand. The financial statements are then prepared from the data furnished by the trial balance and inventory records. Finally, the ledger is closed and the accounts remaining open are balanced.

In the preceding pages the procedure was followed of constructing the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss from the ledger closing. There would be no objection to this plan if the ledger closing and the statement work invariably were required at the same time. Ordinarily, both must be performed at the close of the accounting period. There are, however, occasions during the period when the financial statements are required for such purposes as preparing tax reports, applying for a bank loan, soliciting credit, considering the admission of a partner, offering the business for sale, etc. In the instances cited, the laborious process of closing the ledger would hardly be justified. It becomes necessary, therefore, in the absence of the ledger closing to modify the procedure for drawing up the financial statements. In this connection the trial balance serves a valuable purpose. Additional reasons for having the construction of the financial statements proceed independently of the ledger closing are given on page 172.

The procedure followed in constructing the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss from the trial balance and the inventory records is presented through the following set of models and the accompanying comments. The object is to give the student a working plan that he may follow in drawing up these statements. 163

In the foregoing trial balance and inventory report a code letter appears before each account title and each inventory item, indicating the section in the balance sheet or in the statement of profit and loss in which the item should be con sidered. The code used is: 

Balance Sheet-Asset section Liability section Capital section Statement of Profit and Loss Trading section 

ALC 

Operating Expense section OE 

The preparation of the financial statements consists of two steps, (1) analyzing and labeling the items in the trial balance and the inventory record, and (2) grouping the various items

CONSTRUCTION OF FINANCIAL STATEMENTS 

165 in the appropriate sections in the statements. Before proceeding with the statements themselves, it is necessary to make some explanation of the labeling of some of the items. 

MERCHANDISE STOCK. This item appears both in the trial balance and in the list of inventories. The amount given in the trial balance, $39662.05, represents the cost of the goods on hand at the beginning of the year plus the net purchases during the year. The amount given in the supplementary inventory record, $7420, is the value of the asset at the end of the period. 

The trial balance figure appears as the Total Cost of Stock on the statement of profit and loss and is used to compute the cost of sales. It is necessary, however, to go back to the 

Merchandise Stock account for the supporting data that produce this figure, namely, the stock on hand at the beginning, the subsequent net purchases, and the freight charges on these purchases. The inventory figure as of December 31 is 'used both in the balance sheet to show the value of the asset, and in the statement of profit and loss to compute the cost of sales.

EQUIPMENT ITEMS.-The amount for Delivery Equipment in the trial balance, $1540, represents the cost of this equipment. 

The amount in the inventory list, $1232, is its appraised value at the end of the year. The difference between these two amounts, $308, measures in terms of money the wear and tear sustained through the year. The technical name given to a shrinkage in value resulting from the use of equipment is Depreciation. Thus, there has been a depreciation of $308 on the delivery equipment. Similarly, there has been a depreciation of $68.50 on the furniture and fixtures, being the difference between the cost given in the trial balance, $685, and the appraisal stated in the inventory record of December 31, $616.50. The equipment items in the trial balance are labeled OE, as these figures are used to arrive at the depreciations shown as operating expenses in the statement of profit and loss. The corresponding items in the inventory and valuation report are marked A because they show asset values in the balance sheet, and OE because they, too, are used to determine the depreciations in the statement of profit and loss.

AND FINANCIAL STATEMENTS .-This item also is reported both in the trial balance and on the list of inventories. The trial balance amount, $2869.72, represents the total outlay for services and supplies, and the inventory amount, $75, represents the value of the supplies on hand on December 31 and the services that remain to be rendered. The difference between the amounts in the trial balance and the inventory record, $2794.72, represents the consumption for the year, which appears as an operating expense on the statement of profit and loss. The inventory figure, in addition, appears on the balance sheet, to show the value of the asset at the end of the period.

From these comments two conclusions with regard to the classification of the trial balance and inventory items for the financial statements may be drawn. (1) Every item in the trial balance enters into the construction either of the balance sheet or of the statement of profit and loss. (2) Every item. reported under the head of Inventories and Valuations at the end of the period enters into the construction of both the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss. 

Gathering all these items under their respective heads, we obtain the following statements:

27. LEDGER CLOSING IN RELATION TO THE FINANCIAL STATEMENTS 

Accountants are divided on the question as to whether the closing of the ledger should precede or follow the drawing up of the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss. Those who would close the ledger first argue that the financial statements should be a complete report that reflects the facts set forth in the ledger, and that it is therefore necessary at the end of the business period to record in the proper accounts the new inventories and appraisals before proceeding with the statement work. 

On the other hand the preparation of the statements before closing the ledger has some very definite advantages. First, the statements are not an integral part of the book records and should be made to prove before any attempt is made to bring the ledger to a close. Thus an error made in the statements can be rectified before the permanent record is made in the books. Secondly, the statements serve as a guide in the process of closing and balancing the ledger. Finally, statements are occasionally required during the accounting period and do not at such times involve the closing of the ledger. 

The plan of drawing up the statements before the ledger is closed will be followed in this text. We shall now consider the practical use to which the statements may be put in closing the ledger. The statement of profit and loss furnishes the sequence to be followed in adjusting and closing the accounts. 

To illustrate, let us consider the following statement.

TRADING 

Sales Income

Henry Cross  For the Year Ended December 31, 1932 

$35,000.00

Cost of Goods Sold 

Merchandise Inventory, Jan. 1

Total Cost of Stock Purchases 

Less Inventory, Dec. 31

Cost of Goods Sold 

Gross Profit on Sales

OPERATING EXPENSES Supplies Expense Depreciation on Equipment Total Operating Expense NET PROFIT FOR THE YEAR 

172 $ 8,000.00 24,000.00 $32,000.00 5,000.00 27,000.00 $ 8,000.00 $ 3,000.00 300.00 3,300.00 $ 4,700.00

LEDGER CLOSING AND FINANCIAL STATEMENTS 173 

The steps in closing the ledger conforming to this statement are: 

1. A debit to the Sales Income account and a credit to the Merchandise Stock account for the cost of the goods sold, $27,000. 

2. A debit to Sales Income and a credit to Profit and

Loss for the gross profit on sales, $8000. 

3. A debit to Profit and Loss and a credit to Supplies and Services for consumption, $3000. 

4. A debit to Profit and Loss and a credit to the Equipment account for the depreciation, $300. 

5. A debit to Profit and Loss and a credit to Henry

Cross, Personal or Capital for the net profit for the year, $4700.

This completes the transfer of the capital accounts and the adjustments in the asset accounts for new inventories and appraisals. The accounts remaining open represent the assets, the liabilities, and the proprietor's net capital. The open accounts may now be balanced and the balances verified with the amounts in the balance sheet. As a further test of the accuracy of the work in closing the ledger a trial balance is taken after all the accounts have been closed or balanced. 

This trial balance is commonly referred to as the post-closing trial balance.

Summary 

1. Financial statements may be prepared before or after closing the ledger. 

2. The former procedure is preferable mainly because the statements may be used as a guide in closing and balancing the ledger. 

3. The temporary capital accounts are closed to determine the proprietor's present worth. 

4. After this, the asset accounts, the liability accounts, and the proprietor's capital account are balanced. 

5. A post-closing trial balance is taken to test the accuracy of the ledger closing.

AND FINANCIAL STATEMENTS  

1. Give arguments in favor of preparing the financial statements (a) before closing the ledger, (b) after closing the ledger. 

2. Indicate in their proper order the steps for closing the ledger. 

3. Compare the information in the post-closing trial balance with that in the balance sheet.

PART II 

1. The totals of a trial balance came to $24,358.62 Dr. and $24,120.52 Cr. The bookkeeper checked back his work and detected the following errors: (1) The credit footing of the Sales Income account was 

10c short. (2) A note for $200 issued to a creditor to apply on account was recorded in the creditor's account, but was omitted in the note account. (3) The balance of the Supplies and Services account, $437, was entered in the trial balance as $473. (4) No record was made of a sale on account to T. Bradley amounting to $28.50.

CHAPTER VII  30. SPECIAL MERCHANDISE ACCOUNTS Analysis of the Merchandise Stock Account 

In drawing up the statement of profit and loss it is necessary to analyze the Merchandise Stock account to show the three elements comprising the total cost of stock, namely, the inventory at the start, the net purchases made during the period (total purchases less the returned purchases), and the freight charges on the goods purchased. This analysis becomes involved after the account has been running for some time. To facilitate obtaining the detailed information desired for the profit and loss statement, the Merchandise Stock account may be subdivided into subordinate accounts, in each of which one class of items is recorded. To illustrate, let us consider the following Mer chandise Stock account: 

MERCHANDISE STOCK

Closing the Group of Merchandise Accounts 

When the ledger is to be closed at the end of the business period, the balance of the Freight Inward account is transferred to the Merchandise Purchases account to show the total cost of purchases for the period. The Merchandise Purchases account is then closed by transferring the balance to the Merchandise Inventory account. Finally, as heretofore, the entry for the cost of sales is made, in which the Merchandise Inventory account is credited to show the decrease in the asset Merchandise and the Sales Income account is debited to show the decrease in capital. The following accounts show the closing records for the group of merchandise accounts. The inventory at closing is assumed to be $4520.

31. SUBDIVISION OF THE  ACCOUNT 

The Supplies and Services account contains the records of the cost of the supplies purchased and the services engaged for the conduct of the business. Let us suppose that after a business has been in operation for some time that a statement of profit and loss is prepared and that the following summary is obtained from this statement: 

Sales Income Cost of Goods Sold 

Gross Profit on Sales

Supplies and Services Expense 

Net Profit on Sales $10,000 7,500 $ 2,500 2,000 500 The gross profit on sales, $2,500, is 25% of the sales, $10,000. The expenses, $2,000, are 20% of the sales, and the net profit, $500, is 5% of the sales. It is evident that the small margin of net profit is due to excessive expenses, for, out of a gross profit of 25% on every sale, 20% was used to meet expenses. The business has done slightly better than cover its expenses. To improve this poor showing it will be necessary to cut down on expenses. 

The owner's immediate problem is to investigate the various sources of expense and the amounts of each, so that he may determine where reductions can be made. The Supplies and Services account does not furnish readily the information he seeks. It will be necessary to go through the account' for the entire period and group the various items of supply and service under such heads as rent, salaries, freight and cartage outward, store supplies, office supplies, etc. Had the records been kept in separate accounts for the various kinds of supplies and services, the information desired would have been readily available. Furthermore, it is quite likely that a separate record for each type of supply and service would have enabled the owner to detect any excessive amounts in 190

SUBDIVISION OF  ACCOUNT 191 

time, and would thus have prevented the leakage that caused the profits to dwindle to 5 per cent.

It must be remembered that one of the main objects in keeping accounts is to give information that will be helpful in the efficient management of the business. The business man requires information that is timely and sufficiently detailed to enable him to measure the success of his business and to change its policy when the facts disclosed by the records warrant it. For this reason we find in practice that in place of a general Supplies and Services account, individual accounts are kept for the main items of supply and service that the business requires. Each of these accounts represents one kind of asset purchased. Thus, the Rent account shows the cost of space used, the Salaries account the cost of services rendered by the employees, the Freight and Cartage Outward account the cost of the service in making deliveries to customers. 

Limits to Subdividing Supplies and Services 

A question that arises at this point is how far the subdivision of the Supplies and Services account shall extend. 

Shall there be one account for postage, another for stationery, a third for telephone service, or, assuming that all these are used in the office, shall they be grouped in one account under the head of Office Supplies and Services? No hard and fast rule can be laid down. The answer will be governed by several factors, such as the size of the business, its particular form of organization, and the sums expended for the various items noted. A small dealer may find that one account suffices. In an establishment of considerable size each of the supply and service items mentioned may run into figures that will justify carrying separate accounts.

The aim to keep in mind in any system of recording supplies and services is not to have so few accounts as to cause a lack of adequate information, nor so many as to entail more labor than the information obtained warrants. The average business employs the following supply and service accounts: 

Rent, Salaries, Store Supplies and Services, Office Supplies

and Services, Freight and Cartage Outward, and General 

Supplies and Services. In large organizations additional accounts are kept for Insurance, Light and Heat, Advertising, Telephone and Telegrams, Packing Material, Postage, Car fares, etc.

Closing the Supply and Service Accounts 

The method of closing the individual supply and service accounts at the end of the business period is similar to that presented in an earlier chapter for closing the original Supplies and Services account. At the close of the business period inventories are taken of the supplies on hand and appraisals are made of services paid for but not completely used. The difference between the total of each supply or service account and its inventory or appraisal value represents the amount consumed. This sum is credited to the supply and service account concerned to show the decrease in value of the asset and is debited to Profit and Loss to show the corresponding decrease in capital.

To illustrate the closing of this group of accounts, the following cases will be considered. 

Case I―The Complete Consumption of Supplies and Services 

The Store Supplies and Services account amounts to $182.35 for the year. It represents, mainly, packing material, the supply of which is entirely exhausted at the end of the year. The total sum, therefore, $182.35, will be recorded as a loss.

The Rent account for the year amounts to $1500, covering the use of the business premises to December 31, the date of closing. This service is completely used up and its cost, $1500, will be treated as a loss for the period. 

The account records after closing are:

Summary 1. The subdivision of the Supplies and Services account into special accounts furnishes more detailed information that is helpful for efficiently managing the business. 

2. At the close of the business period each of the special 

Supply and Service accounts is adjusted by crediting it with the amount of the supply or service that has been consumed. 

The Profit and Loss account is correspondingly debited with the same amount for the expense incurred.

CHAPTER VIII  33. THE JOURNAL Limitations of the Ledger 

Up to this point the ledger has been used as the sole book of record of the business. The information in it has been classified in the accounts and summarized in the statement of profit and loss and in the balance sheet at the close of the business period to determine the progress made and the financial condition reached.

The ledger, however, as a book of record, has its limitations. 

In the first place, the double entry record of every transaction is spread over at least two accounts. This separation of the record into its debit and credit elements makes it exceedingly difficult for one going over the ledger to ascertain the exact nature of the business events. For example, if a debit item of $1000 appears in the Cash account, one cannot tell whether this represents a cash sale of merchandise, or the payment of a note receivable, or an investment by the proprietor, or a loan made to the business by an outside party, or a payment on account by a customer. 

To determine just what the transaction was that resulted in the increase of $1000 cash, it is necessary to go through all the accounts until a credit item of $1000 is found under the same date as that shown in the Cash record.

In the second place, the ledger fails to show the business events in the order in which they occur. Thus, if the owner wishes to know what business was transacted during the week of January 5th to 10th, inclusive, it is necessary to go through the ledger and select such items as were entered in the various accounts between the two dates given. 

Another objection arising from the method of recording directly in the ledger is that errors are apt to be made in the entries in 205

the accounts and that these cannot be readily detected in checking back the ledger after the trial balance has been prepared. 

Let us suppose, for example, that a sale on account amounting to $78 has been made, and that the customer's account has been charged, in error, with $87, while the Sales Income account has been properly credited with $78. The trial balance will be out by $9. To detect the error it will be necessary to check every item in the ledger against its offsetting item in some other account until a pair of items is found that shows the discrepancy. Such a task is not only laborious and time-consuming, but in any fair sized business almost impossible to achieve. 

Introduction of the Journal 

To meet these objections, an additional book of record, called the Journal, is used. The transactions are recorded in the journal as they occur, and the entries are then carried from the journal to the ledger, where they are distributed to their appropriate accounts. For this reason the journal is known as the book of original entry. The process of entering the records in the journal is referred to as journalizing. Each journal entry is a complete record of a transaction, giving the date, the accounts to be debited and credited, the sums of money involved, and a brief and clear description of the event. This information is shown in the model journal on page 207. 

This model shows in the order of their occurrence the transactions in which Thomas Snedden engaged during his first week in business. The journal opens with a statement of Thomas 

Snedden's going into business, showing his investment and the accounts to which it gives rise. This is followed by an entry recording the payment of rent to the Downer Realty Co. A purchase of store and office fixtures is next recorded showing the manner of payment. The next transaction on April 5 is a purchase of merchandise of the Spaulding Garment Co. on account. 

On the 6th a sale of merchandise to Andrew Schaffner on account is set forth in detail. An entry for the payment for wrapping material completes the business record for the week. 

The figures in the column at the left are inserted when the transfer of the record is made from the journal to the ledger. 

The method of making this transfer is explained on pages 209 and 211.

The explanation, describing the transaction

Column (a) marked L.F. (ledger folio) indicates the pages or folios in the ledger on which the debit and the credit accounts appear.

Column (b) contains the date in the middle of the first line, the account to be debited on the second line, the account to be credited indented on the third line, and, finally, the explanation, describing briefly the transaction.

Column (c) states the amount with which the "debit" account is to be charged. 

Column (d) states the amount to be credited to the "credit" account.

The only variation in the arrangement of these details in the model journal is in the first entry, where the explanation precedes the double entry record. The order is reversed to give prominence to the introductory statement announcing the commencement of business.

The entry of April 3 contains one debit equal in amount to the sum of the two offsetting credits. An entry that contains more than one debit or more than one credit is known as a compound entry.

The purchase entry of the 5th does not give a detailed record of the items purchased because such information is readily obtainable from the purchase invoice on file. The sales record of the 6th, however, gives the particulars for each of the items sold. This is necessary as the sales invoice from which the entry is made is sent to the customer. Where a duplicate copy of the invoice is made out and retained by the firm, the itemized part of the sales record is omitted in the explanation.

After the journal entries have been made, they must be transferred to the ledger. This operation is known as posting. SALES INCOME 

(Page 10) 19Apr.

J 1 977 50 The operation of posting calls for comment. The first step in the process consists of carrying the debit item in the journal entry to the corresponding account in the ledger, the following information being written on the debit side of the account: (a) the date, (b) the amount, (c) the journal page in the folio column to the left of the amount column, and (d) in the explanation column the symbol "J", representing the original book of entry from which the record comes. The ledger page on which this account appears is then entered in the folio column in the journal, directly in front of the debit item that has been posted. This figure indicates that the posting has been completed. The placing of this figure in the journal folio column is known as post-marking.

The credit item in the journal entry is then treated in a similar way. The bookkeeper then proceeds to post the remaining journal entries in order. Some bookkeepers find that they can work with more speed and greater accuracy by posting all the debit items of the journal entries first, and then proceeding with the credit items.

In the customers' and the creditors' accounts the terms of the invoice may be inserted in the explanation columns when the postings are made. Similarly, in the note accounts the term for which the paper is to run may be indicated. This information is helpful in following up in these accounts the payments as they fall due.

The careful and efficient bookkeeper keeps his ledger up to date by posting daily from his journal. This is especially desirable in the handling of customers' and creditors' accounts,

as the bookkeeper is constantly called upon at a moment's notice to furnish information relative to these accounts. Furthermore, in these accounts as well as in the note accounts, the payment of the sums due is an important consideration and requires careful supervision. The records should, therefore, always be complete. The following illustration shows the necessity for keeping accounts up to date. J. Bruce, a customer, has a credit privilege up to $2000. His account on February 10 shows a balance of $1850. We receive his order for $500, but decline to fill it as this would exceed the limit of credit extended to him. Later it is discovered that his cash remittance of $800 was received on February 9, but that the bookkeeper delayed posting the item from the journal. Had this posting been made promptly, Bruce's account on February 10 would have shown a debit balance of $650 and his order for merchandise would not have been held up. 

The Journal as an Aid in Preventing and Detecting Error 

Reference has already been made to the possibilities of error if the plan is followed of making the entries directly in the accounts without any preliminary record. The analysis of every transaction into its debit and credit elements in the journal and the added precaution of post-marking these after the postings have been made go a long way toward the prevention of error in the ledger. It remains to be shown how the journal can be effectively used to detect errors after the trial balance has been taken.

Where a bookkeeper takes a trial balance and fails to strike a balance, he proceeds to retrace his steps to locate the error in the order listed below: 

1. The trial balance is re-added. 

2. The figures in the trial balance are checked against the pencil balances in the accounts, three types of error in the trial balance being kept in mind,—(1) omission of an item, (2) entering a wrong amount, and (3) entering in the wrong column. 

3. The balance in each account is verified by going over the additions and the subtraction in the account.

THE JOURNAL 

213 4. Finally, the postings in the ledger are checked back against the journal entries. The following plan for systematically checking back the ledger is suggested: (1) Begin with the first account in the ledger and check each debit against its entry in the journal, placing a small pencil check at the left of the amount in the ledger and similarly in the journal. (2) Proceed to check back the debit side of the accounts that follow, in their regular order. (3) Start again with the first account and check back all the credits. Continue in the same way until all the accounts have been covered. (4) Inspect the journal carefully to see if it contains any unchecked items.

During the process of checking back from the ledger to the journal, be on the lookout for the following common types of error: (1) posting to the wrong side of the account, (2) posting to the wrong account, (3) transposition of figures, e.g., writing $87 for $78, (4) error in decimal notation, e.g., writing $4.80 for $480 (this is known as a slide), and (5) omission of a posting, as evidenced by an unchecked item in the journal. 

The following illustration shows the checking of two accounts against the entry record in the journal. 19JOURNAL January 3, 1915 24 2 Cash

Summary 

1. The journal is used as a book of original entry in which the complete entry for each transaction is recorded, setting forth the date, the accounts, the amounts involved, and the explanation. 

2. The entries in the journal are posted to the corresponding accounts in the ledger. 

3. The journal serves (a) as a historical record, (b) as a medium for making orderly postings to the ledger, (c) as a means of checking the account records when the trial balance indicates that there is an error in the ledger.

34.  Purpose of Adjustment and Transfer Entries 

It was shown in earlier lessons that the closing of the ledger at the end of a business period accomplishes two distinct purposes, (1) the adjustment of accounts for changes that have occurred during the period but that have not been recorded, and (2) the closing of temporary accounts by the transfer of their balances to permanent accounts. An illustration of an adjustment record is the entry "Profit and Loss to Supplies and Services, $175." Here are recorded the decrease in value in the asset Supplies and Services and the decrease in capital resulting from the consumption of supplies and services during the period. An illustration of a transfer record is the entry "Sales Income to Profit and Loss, $850.” In this entry, the Sales Income account is closed, the balance being transferred as a profit to the Profit and Loss account. 

As there are many accounts that must be either adjusted or transferred to close the ledger, it is advisable for several reasons that the adjustment and transfer records be entered in the journal before they are made in the ledger. This preliminary step of journalizing the closing entries enables the bookkeeper to arrange the adjustments and the transfers in their proper order, and lessens the chances of error in closing the ledger. Another reason for closing the ledger through the journal is that it makes the journal the source for practically all the records in the ledger. Every debit and credit ledger item can be traced back to the journal, where a complete explanation can be found for the record. Furthermore, if an error is committed in closing the ledger, it can be readily detected by carefully checking the postings against the original entries in the journal. 

The following statement will be used as a basis for closing the ledger through the journal: 221

Treatment of Inventory Items 

In the foregoing record, the entry "Merchandise Inventory (New) to Merchandise Inventory (Old)," precedes the entry for the cost of goods sold. This furnishes in the Merchandise Inventory account the figures for deriving the cost of goods sold. In posting the inventory entry, the debit is written in the new section of the account to show the value of the stock at the beginning of the next period, and the credit is placed in the old section to close the current account. 

The furniture appraisal and the supply inventories are treated in a similar way. The entries for the valuation of the assets are set up to determine the various operating expenses consumed. The debit of each entry is posted to the new section of the asset account and the credit to the old section. 

These inventory entries are simply transfer entries for the purpose of closing the current accounts and setting up the valuations for the next period. For this reason the debit postings are dated in the ledger as of the beginning of the new period. It is not necessary, however, to make journal entries for setting up the new inventories and appraisals at the close of the period. The same result may be accomplished by balancing the accounts affected after the adjustments have been made for any decreases in the value of the assets. Thus, in the illustration on pages 222 and 223 the optional entries are indicated by an asterisk (*). The following accounts show the ledger closing by journal entry. The amounts shown parenthetically represent the balances in the accounts prior to closing. 19Sept. 30

Summary 

1. To close the ledger two types of entry are made in the journal: (1) the adjustment entry and (2) the transfer entry. 

2. Adjustment entries are made to record changes that have occurred during the period but that have not been recorded in the accounts. 

3. Transfer entries are made for the purpose of closing their accounts by transferring balances to other accounts in the same group. 

4. New inventories and appraisals may be set up either by means of journal transfer entries or by direct balancing of the accounts after all adjustments have been made.

35. THE SALES JOURNAL Subdivision of the Journal 

The transactions of a business are recorded in the journal in the order in which they occur. Such a record is consequently miscellaneous in character. Some of the entries have the same debit or the same credit because they represent transactions of a similar nature. For example, the entries for sales all have the same credit-Sales Income; the entries for purchases of merchandise have a common debit-Merchandise Purchases; the entries for the receipts of cash from one source or another have a common debit-Cash; and the entries recording cash payments have a common credit-Cash. If instead of writing these entries in the journal as the transactions occur, they were classified according to a common debit or a common credit and each group recorded in a separate journal, it would not be necessary to repeat in each entry the debit or the credit element that is common to the group.

Advantages of Subdividing the Journal 

The economy resulting from a subdivision of the journal will be apparent from a comparison of the following two parallel sets of sales entries shown in skeleton form. 

1. Entering in the journal 

H. Conboy

January 21, 19 H. Conboy 250 24

A. Reeves 480 29

Sales Income, Cr. 1280 

In the second form, headed "Sales Journal," the credit item "Sales Income," common to all the entries, is omitted until the end of the month, when the debits are added and one credit entry, "Sales Income $1280," is made for the total sales. 

Not only is there a saving in entry work by using a special journal for the sales, but a corresponding reduction in the number of postings is effected in the ledger. The following two sets of ledger records, resulting from the two groups of entry records shown above, illustrate this saving in posting to the Sales Income account.

1/21 J1 1/24 J1 1/26 $250 1/31 SJ1 $1280 1/29 J1 ===ཋ 160 J1 480 390 The separation of sales entries from the rest of the journal is presented here to illustrate the economy resulting from the subdivision of the journal. Similar subdivisions are made for merchandise purchase entries and for entries involving the receipts and payments of cash. This gives rise to the employment of additional special journals, which are presented in detail in sections 36 and 37 of this chapter.

Another advantage in having these special journals is that it becomes possible to set more than one bookkeeper at work on the books. While entries are being made in one journal, the entries from another journal are being posted. This arrangement is quite necessary in practice, as the volume of work is frequently more than one bookkeeper can handle. In some offices the work is divided so that one bookkeeper, the entry clerk, has charge of all the journals, and another, the ledger clerk, makes all the postings. Under another arrangement frequently followed, each bookkeeper is responsible for a particular journal and the postings in the ledger from that journal.

Two styles of sales journals are illustrated here. In the first, the items of sale are set forth in each entry, making the record a duplicate of the invoice sent to the customer. It should be noted that the two money columns are no longer debit and credit columns. The first money column contains the extensions, which are the amounts charged for the individual items sold. 

The second column shows the total amount of the sale to be charged to the customer. The debit postings from the sales entries to the individual customers' accounts are made from day to day. The Sales Income account is credited at the end of the month with the total sales. Only sales made on account are entered in the sales journal. Cash sales will be considered in a later section.

1. When transactions of the same type occur frequently, it is economical to make the original entries in a separate journal so that at the end of the month one entry may be made for the debit or credit account common to the group.

36. THE PURCHASE JOURNAL 

The principle of specialization of the journal may now be extended to entries for merchandise purchases on account. The journal employed for this purpose is called the Purchase Journal. 

As in the case of the sales journal, the purchase journal is a labor-saving device, since the debit entry to Merchandise Purchases is made in a lump sum at the close of the month in place of individual debits for the purchases made. A corresponding economy is effected in the ledger by making a single posting in the Merchandise Purchases account for the total purchases. The use of the purchase journal facilitates also the distribution of the work between two or more bookkeepers. 

The form of purchase journal generally employed is the 

Purchase Register, which, like the Sales Register, gives for each transaction the data that are required for posting. No record is made of the individual items of purchase, as this information is readily available from the purchase invoices on file. 

Purchase Journal Date L. F.

21 Merchandise turchase. Dr. 229655 In the model purchase journal the date of entry does not coincide with the date of invoice. The former represents the date when the purchase invoice has been checked against the goods received. The invoice date must be recorded as the due date of each invoice is determined by adding the number of days 238

THE PURCHASE JOURNAL 

239 in the credit period to the date of invoice; thus, in the first entry the date of invoice, January 2, plus the term of credit, 30 days, fixes the due date at February 1.

The invoices are numbered consecutively as they are entered and are frequently filed in numerical order until they are paid. Sometimes, the invoice number column is used to record the seller's invoice number. This information is desirable when, in communicating with the seller, reference has to be made to the invoice in question. 

In posting from the purchase journal, all the data should be transferred to the creditor's account. This is illustrated in the posting of the first entry.

37.  

With the sales entries and the purchase entries removed to special journals, there still remains in the general journal a variety of entries, many of which involve the item Cash. These may be divided into two groups, those representing receipts of cash and those representing payments of cash. If each of these groups were treated independently in a special journal, the labor-saving features observed in the use of the sales and the purchase journals would be repeated here. If, for example, ten cash transactions had taken place, five involving cash receipts and five involving cash payments, ten postings to the Cash account would be required if these transactions were passed through the general journal. If, however, the receipts were recorded in a special journal, one debit journal entry to Cash for the total receipts would suffice, resulting in one debit posting to the Cash account. Similarly, the recording of the five payments in a separate journal would reduce the credit-· ing of the Cash account to a single posting through a credit entry in the special journal for the total payments. The treatment of cash entries in special journals is illustrated in the following models.

Posting from the Cash Journals

The plan followed is similar to the method of posting from the sales and the purchase journals. Daily postings from the cash receipts journal are made to the credit side of the accounts indicated and at the end of the month the Cash account is debited for the total cash receipts. Similarly, daily postings are made from the cash payments journal to the debit side of the accounts indicated and at the end of the month the Cash account is credited for the total payments. Thus, the Cash account, which heretofore had as many postings as there were cash transactions, now has but two postings each month to show the total receipts and the total payments. 

The Cash Book 

The two cash journals are closely related to each other, since both are used for recording cash transactions. As the daily cash receipts and the total cash payments are not recorded in the Cash account until the cash journals are summarized at the end of the month, it is necessary at all other times to refer to the cash journals for information pertaining to cash. The information most frequently desired is the actual

amount of cash on hand at a particular moment. To furnish such information readily the two sets of cash records are usually kept collaterally in one book, called the Cash Book. 

The cash receipts entries are made on the left-hand page and opposite to these, on the right-hand page the entries for cash payments are recorded. By footing in pencil the amount column on each side daily the cash balance is always easily obtainable. Although both cash journals are combined into one book, it must be remembered that each side of the cash book constitutes a special journal and is an independent and complete double entry record.

Summarizing and Balancing the Cash Book 

At the end of the month the cash book is closed by (1) carrying the footing of each amount column into the total column, (2) entering the cash balance on the payment side, (3) footing the total columns on the same line across both pages, (4) ruling off across the date and the money columns, and (5) bringing the balance down for the next month. The method of summarizing and balancing the cash book is shown in the model on pages 246 and 247. 

Opening Entry for Investment of Cash and Other Assets 

In opening a set of books accounts are set up for the various assets, the liabilities, if any, and for the capital by means of an opening entry in the General Journal. In practically every case cash will be one of the assets invested. The question arises as to whether the record of cash should appear in the General Journal or in the Cash Receipts Journal. The method to be followed for recording the cash will depend on whether cash is the only asset invested or one of several assets invested. In the first case an opening statement in the 

General Journal accompanied by an entry for the cash investment in the Cash Receipts Journal is made. These records are illustrated on the next page.

Other Special Journals 

In addition to the general journal, three special books of original entry-the sales book, the purchase book and the cash book-have been considered. These four books constitute the journals generally employed in double entry sets of books. The list of special journals is not limited, however, to those studied in this chapter. In a business, for instance, that receives many notes, from its customers, it is economical to use a notes receivable journal in place of entering the receipts of notes in the general journal. In fact, the special set of journals that a business requires is determined entirely by the frequency of certain types of transactions. The books employed must always be adapted to the particular needs of the business. The student will meet other illustrations of the special journal in his later studies. 

Summary 

1. All cash receipts and payments are recorded in special journals called the Cash Receipts Journal and the Cash Payments Journal. 

2. The two sets of cash records are kept usually in one book called the Cash Book. 

3. Daily credit postings are made from the Cash Receipts

Journal to the accounts indicated and at the end of the month the total of the receipts is posted as a debit to the Cash account. 

4. Daily debit postings are made from the Cash Payments Journal to the accounts indicated and at the end of the month the total of the payments is posted as a credit to the 

Cash account. 

5. At the end of the month, the Cash Book is balanced and ruled off. 

6. The cash investment is entered in the Cash Receipts 

Journal when it is the only asset invested. If there are in addition other assets, the entire opening entry is made in the General Journal and the cash on hand is shown as a bal ance in the Cash Receipts Journal.

CHAPTER IX  39.  

The object of this chapter is to familiarize the student with the form of the more commonly used business papers and to give him some practice in employing them as source material for the bookkeeping records. These papers constitute the documentary evidence on which all records made in the books are based. 

Some of these business forms, such as the check, the order for goods, the invoice, the promissory note, and the statement of account, have been presented in earlier chapters in connection with related bookkeeping topics. Before proceeding with an application of these papers to a business set, a brief treatment of a few additional forms will be presented. 

The Credit Memorandum 

It is sometimes necessary, after a sale has been made on account, for the seller to credit the customer's account because some of the goods have been returned or because an allowance has to be made for a claim submitted by the customer. Such claim may grow out of one of the following causes: (1) defective or inferior quality of merchandise, (2) a shortage in the quantity delivered, (3) an overcharge in price, (4) an error in calculation on the invoice. It is not considered good practice for a customer to alter the sales invoice when he returns part of the goods or makes a claim for an allowance. The written approval of the creditor authorizing the adjustment sought should be obtained. 

For this purpose a form known as a Credit Memorandum is used. 

It is usually prepared in duplicate, one copy going to the customer 259

CREDIT MEMORANDUM HORNE & ROGERS Electrical Lighting Fixtures 

275 Broad Street

No. 248 NEWARK, N. J. 

Aug. 8, 192 

THE ACCOUNT OF Henry L. Sommers 

Battle Creek, Mich.

Approved by 

Wright 

IS CREDITED AS FOLLOWS: 

Manager Invoice

July 28 1 Lighting Fixture #382 returned 22 50 and the other being retained by the creditor. From it the journal entry is prepared to make the necessary adjustment. 

In form the credit memorandum resembles the sales invoice, setting forth the parties concerned, the particulars about the return or the allowance, and the amount in question. A serial number is used so that every credit bill issued can be accounted for. In order that there may be no irregularities in crediting customers' accounts, the written approval of the manager or some other duly authorized person is required. Generally the sales invoice and the credit memorandum are different in color and size so that they may be easily distinguished from each other. 

The Receipt 

Payments are usually made by check or in currency. In the case of the former, the check eventually comes back to the drawer (the one who issued the check), marked "Paid" and serves as evidence of payment. When, however, a payment is made in currency, it must be evidenced in some other way.

The common method is to stamp the bill "Paid," thereby converting it into a receipted bill. The form generally used is: Per APR. 5, 1926 

L. WISSENER & SON 

In the absence of a bill, or where a separate receipt is requested, the following form is used. The stub is retained by the one issuing the receipt as his record. 

Date 

Apr. 21926 To 

Donald Renault RECEIVED OF 

Boston, Mass.

Aps.2.1926 

Donald Renault on Rent of stere Leventy five and in For for April for sent of stores for April 1926 $75,00 $75100 By 

BIRWACK REALTY CORP. 

Dollars 

Nathaniel Bronson Treasurer

Opening a Checking Account 

A person wishing to open a check account at a commercial bank must be introduced by a depositor or some other responsible individual, so that the institution may be satisfied as to the personal integrity of the prospective depositor. The applicant writes his name on a signature card in the style in which he intends to sign checks and other negotiable instruments that will be paid through the bank. This signature card is kept on file at the bank for comparison when any doubt arises as to the genuineness of the depositor's signature on a check or note. Banks take this precaution as they are held liable for any payment made on a forged signature.

The depositor is now ready to make his first deposit. He fills out a deposit slip similar to the following form, and hands it in with the cash funds to the receiving teller. 

BANK OF COMMERCE DEPOSITED BY 

Charles Abbot

Jan 2 1926 

PLEASE LIST EACH CHECK SEPARATELY 

Dollars Cents COIN 1635 

BILLS CHECKS 

30760 9742 Philadelphia 8:5176 139813 

SAFE DEPOSIT VAULTS 

The deposit is entered by the receiving teller in a pass book which is given to the depositor and serves as his receipt for the funds turned into the bank. Every subsequent deposit must be accompanied by a deposit slip and the pass book. The record made in the pass book is shown in the illustration on page 263. 

At the end of every month the depositor receives his cancelled checks back from the bank accompanied by a statement of his account. This form and the method of checking it against the depositor's records are treated at length in Chapter XIII.

WITH Charles Abbot IN ACCOUNT BANK OF COMMERCE 

JAN 2 1926 1926 LF. 139713 

Preparing the Bank Deposit 

In preparing the bank deposit, the following procedure should be observed: 

1. All coins should be arranged in stacks by denominations and should then be put up in separate packages. The name of the depositor and the amount contained should appear on the outside of each package. Banks usually supply the depositor with wrappers. The number of coins in each denomination that these packages should contain is: 50 cents, 40 nickels ($2), 50 dimes ($5), 40 quarters ($10), 20 half-dollars ($10), 10 silver dollars ($10). 

2. All bills should be turned face up with the smallest denomination on top. 

3. All checks should be endorsed by the depositor, care being taken to see that the signature in the indorsement corresponds exactly to the name given for the payee on the face of the check. It is not essential that the indorsement be written. 

It is usually rubber-stamped where the number of checks deposited is large. Either of the following forms of indorsement should be used in preference to the blank indorsement:

Pay to the order of 

For deposit in

THE THE BANK OF COMMERCE or BANK OF COMMERCE 

Charles Abbot 

Charles Abbot The checks should then be arranged face up, checks on city banks coming first, followed by out-of-town checks. 

4. The deposit slip should then be filled out. Each check should be listed separately. In the case of out-of-town checks, the city of the bank on which the check is drawn should be indicated in the margin to the left of the money column. Deposit slips are supplied by the bank. 

5. A copy of the deposit should be made on the back of the stub in the check book and the total entered on the opposite side. The method of entering the deposits, the checks drawn and the bank balance in the check book is shown in the following illustration:

BACK OF STUB 

Jan 15 Deposit

FACE OF STUB

To Bracy & Co

No. 2466

For week ending 117 Amount

6. It is the practice of a good many bookkeepers and cashiers not to send any deposit to the bank until they have made certain that the cash book records are complete and in accord with the cash funds on hand and the balance at the bank. 

The method of establishing this fact is known as proving cash, and the form drawn up is known as the proof of cash. This form is shown in the following illustration:

CHARLES ABBOT PROOF OF CASH 

151926

Summary 

1. When an amount charged to a customer on a sales invoice is to be reduced because of a return of merchandise, a shortage in delivery, an overcharge in price, or an error in calculation, a credit memorandum is sent to the customer for the amount involved. 

2. Checks that are finally returned by the bank marked "Paid" serve as evidence of payment. When payments are made in currency, a receipted bill or a separate receipt should be obtained from the payee as evidence of payment. 

3. The routine for opening a checking account consists of (a) introduction of the new depositor by a responsible individual, (b) preparation of the signature card, (c) filling out of a deposit slip, and (d) receiving a check book and pass book on making the deposit. 

4. The steps in preparing a bank deposit consist of (a) stacking coins, (b) arranging bills by denominations, (c) indorsing all checks, (d) filling out a deposit slip, (e) entering the deposit on the check stub, (f) preparing a proof of cash, and (g) depositing at the bank and having the entry made in the pass book.

1. List the items of information that are contained in each of the following business forms: (a) a check, (b) a sales invoice, (c) a receipted bill for office supplies, (d) a rent receipt, (e) a monthly statement of a customer's account, (f) a credit memorandum for goods returned to a creditor, (g) a note received from a customer. 

2. Describe the procedure for opening a checking account. 

3. Name five causes for sending a customer a credit memorandum. 

4. Why is it important that a credit memorandum shall have the approval of the manager?

6. State the steps to be followed in preparing a bank deposit. 

7. Arthur L. Braunstein received a check from a customer made out to A. Brownstein. Show how this check will be indorsed for deposit at the bank. 

8. Describe the use of the check stub as a record of transactions with the bank.

40.  

The following set is designed to be worked in connection with the business papers involved in the transactions. Complete data are given in the text for those wishing to dispense with the business papers and to treat the set solely as a bookkeeping exercise. However, it is urged that the business papers accompanying the set be employed, so that the student may get some practical training in handling and writing these papers and in compiling the bookkeeping records directly from them. 

The publishers have put these papers up in a separate package, labelled Outfit No. 1. 

Business papers may be classified as incoming and outgoing. 

In the first class are those received from sources outside of the business, such, for example, as purchase invoices, and checks and notes received from customers. The incoming papers for the set are arranged in convenient book form. As a transaction involving an incoming paper is considered, the form should be removed from the "incoming" book and should be used as the source material for making the record in the appropriate book of original entry. The paper should then be filed in accordance with instructions given in the text. 

The outgoing forms are those that originate in the business and are sent out after the bookkeeping records have been made. 

Examples of these are checks and notes to creditors, sales invoices, credit memoranda to customers, bank deposit slips, etc. 

A blank check book and a package of miscellaneous blanks are part of the equipment of Outfit No. 1. The student should fill out every outgoing paper and prepare the original entry directly from it. The outgoing paper should then be placed in the envelope marked "Outgoing Papers."

Directions for Posting 

No. 17. Post all the entries to date, observing the following arrangement in the ledger: 

1. List the accounts in the ledger in the order in which they come in the journals. 

2. Allow one-fourth of the page for each account. 3. 

Use pages 1 to 5 inclusive for miscellaneous accounts. 

4. Reserve page 6 for accounts with trade creditors, and page 7 for accounts with customers. 

5. Number the accounts on each page a, b, c and d respectively. Thus, the accounts on page 1 will be numbered 1a, 1b, 1c and 1d.

CHAPTER X  41. INTEREST COST The Nature of Interest 

In our study of accounts for supply and service assets, it was pointed out that all charges in such accounts represent the cost of acquiring supplies and services necessary for operating the business. It was also brought out in an earlier chapter that the element of credit is present to a very large extent in the affairs of the average business man. Both of these considerations must be kept in mind in inquiring into the nature of interest.

When a business man has insufficient funds with which to finance his business, he is obliged to borrow money from some outside source. The use of this borrowed money represents a money service for which a compensation must be paid. The borrower is prepared to meet this cost in the same sense that he stands ready to pay for the use of floor space or for the labor he employs. Interest is the term used to designate the cost of the use of money, or, as it is frequently expressed, interest is the cost of credit. 

Accounting for Interest 

Problem: (a) Mar. 1, student borrowed $600 from J. Levitas, giving him a promissory note due in 60 days and bearing interest at 6 per cent. (b) Apr. 30, student paid J. Levitas $606 for the note and the interest due.

It should be observed that the note issued to the student bears interest, thus differing from the non-interest bearing notes previously studied. Although cash loans may be made on the verbal promise of the borrower, it is the common prac- 281

tice for the borrower to give his promissory note as evidence of his liability. The form of an interest-bearing note is shown in the following illustration: 

No. 6f 

Cleveland, Or Fete 12, 19Sixty days after date the order of Noward Rogalin 

Six Hundred 2000 promise to pay to payable at the Liberty National Bank $6000

Dollars 

Value received with interest at 6% per annum Due Apr.1419 

John F. Slates 

In the transaction of Mar. 1, the student's asset Cash and his liability Notes Payable are each increased by $600. The two accounts involved are Cash and Notes Payable, and the entry, expressed in journal form, is: Cash to Notes Payable, $600. 

The note is due Apr. 30, at which time the student's liability to J. Levitas is $606, comprising $600 on the note and $6 for the interest. The liability has mounted from $600 to $606 from

Rules for Debiting and Crediting the Interest Cost Account INTEREST COST 

Debit Interest Cost for the cost of the use of money. 

Credit Interest Cost for the consumption of the money service rendered.

INTEREST COST 

285 

Summary 

1. Interest is a service asset representing the use by one person of money belonging to another. 

2. Interest Cost is the title of the account for recording the acquisition and the consumption of money service. 

3. An interest-bearing note calls for the payment of the face value of the note plus a service charge (interest) for the use of this sum for a stated period. 

4. At the periodic closing of the books, the Interest Cost account is credited for the money service consumed and the Profit and Loss account is debited for the corresponding expense incurred.

Income Accounts 42. INTEREST INCOME 

The main source of a merchant's income is derived from the sale of merchandise. There may be, however, other sources of income. For example, a merchant may sublet some of his floor space and receive a payment for rent. Again, he may, in addition to selling his own goods, handle another concern's line on a commission basis. In each of the instances cited, the compensation received by the merchant increases his asset Cash and his capital. Special capital accounts, similar to the Sales Income account, are employed to show these increases in capital. Thus, on receipt of the rent payment, the merchant's entry is "Cash to Rent Income, $ -." Similarly the compensation received as commission for selling goods results in the entry "Cash to Income from Commissions, $- Capital accounts representing earnings of the business for services rendered are known as Service 

Income accounts. 

Interest as a Source of Income

In the same way that interest is a source of cost to the business when it borrows money, interest may be a source of income when the business loans money to others or extends credit to them for a compensation. Two examples will illustrate how interest income is derived. 

1. On Jan. 5, B. Trent loaned C. Gray $1200 for one month on the latter's note, bearing interest at 6 per cent. At maturity 

Gray paid his note and the interest due, $1206.

At the time the loan was made, Trent's asset Cash was decreased and his asset Notes Receivable was increased by $1200. 

When the note was paid, his asset Cash was increased by $1206 and his asset Notes Receivable was decreased by $1200, resulting in an increase of $6 in his capital. This example illustrates the earning of interest on a loan. As in the case of Interest Cost, it is not practical to show the interest earned each day. Instead, the record is postponed until the payment is received. The special capital account for showing the interest earned is the 

Interest Income account. 289

Trent's books are:
