43. MERCHANDISE DISCOUNT Trade Discount 

Manufacturers and wholesale distributors supply their dealers with catalogues to advertise their merchandise and to furnish information required in ordering goods. These catalogues contain photographic illustrations, specifications describing the articles carried, their catalogue numbers and list prices. It would be very costly to issue a new catalogue every time new prices had to be put into effect. To meet this need, a discount sheet is furnished with the catalogue. Whenever a change in price is made, a revised discount sheet is substituted for the old For example, a desk may be listed in a furniture catalogue at $60. On the discount sheet this particular number may be quoted at a discount of 33%, bringing the actual invoice price down to $40. If later, it is decided to reduce the sale price to $36, a new discount sheet will be issued quoting discount rates of 33% and 10% on this item. Discount rates applied to the list price are known as Trade Discounts. one.

The method of arriving at the invoice price is shown in the following calculation: $60, list price 

33% of $60 = $20, the first discount (1) (2) (3) (4) $60-$20 

10% of $40 $40, list price less first discount $4, the second discount (5) $40-$4 $36, the invoice price.

Retailers frequently have their customers make their selections from the catalogue, as it is impossible to carry a complete line in stock. Using a list price in the catalogue enables the dealer to withhold from the customer the dealer's actual cost of the article. Thus, using the example quoted above, a customer consulting the catalogue will see the desk listed at $60, whereas the dealer's purchase price is $36.

As the trade discount is only a means of arriving at the invoice price of an article, neither the list price nor the discount 297

offered is reflected in any of the bookkeeping records of the seller or the buyer. Thus, in the foregoing illustration, the sales record of the manufacturer will show the desk sold at $36, and the purchase record of the retailer will show his cost to be $36. In neither record will any mention be made of the list price or the trade discount.

Cash Discount 

This is a discount on merchandise offered in the terms of sale in order to induce the buyer to pay before the full credit period has elapsed. Thus, if the terms in a sale of an article for $50 are 2/10, n/30 (2 per cent in 10 days, or the net amount in 30 days), the buyer has a choice of paying the full amount, $50, 30 days after the date of purchase, or $49 ($50 less 2%) within the 

10 days following the date of purchase. Two reasons prompt the seller to offer this cash discount. First, it serves as an inducement to the customer to make a prompt settlement of his indebtedness. This has the effect of cutting down materially the losses firms sustain from uncollectible accounts that have become overdue. In the second place, the conversion of accounts receivable into cash supplies ready funds with which to finance the business. The seller is willing to reduce his profit on the sale in order to secure these working funds before the full term of credit has expired. The discount allowed on the sale for payment before the credit period has expired is referred to as a Discount on Sales from the standpoint of the seller, and as a Discount on Purchases from the standpoint of the buyer. 

Treatment of Discount on Sales 

Problem: Oct. 5, M. Bahret sold a bill of goods to H. Dean for $500, terms: 2/10, n/30.

Oct. 15, H. Dean gave his check for $490 to M. Bahret in payment of invoice of Oct. 5, less 2 per cent. 

The records in M. Bahret's ledger for the sale are: 

H. DEAN 

19Oct. 5 $500 

SALES INCOME 

19Oct. 5 $500

MERCHANDISE DISCOUNT 

299 When the cash is received, Bahret's asset Cash is increased by $490 and his asset Accounts Receivable is decreased by $500. 

There is a net decrease in assets of $10 which results in a decrease in capital of $10. This capital decrease is shown in a capital account called "Discount on Sales." The account records for the remittance on Oct. 15 are: 

H. DEAN

CASH 

19Oct. 15 $500 

Oct. 15 $490 19DISCOUNT ON Sales 

19Oct. 15 $10 The original entry on Bahret's books for the cash settlement on Oct. 15 will now be considered. Journalizing the account records shown above for Oct. 15, we have Cash 

Discount on Sales

H. Dean 

$490 10 $500 

As the transaction represents a receipt of cash, it follows that the entry should be made in the Cash Receipts journal. Here, a difficulty arises. If the amount received, $490, is entered, H. Dean's account will be credited with $490 instead of $500, leaving a balance of $10 in the account. This is wrong, as H. 

Dean's check has been accepted in full settlement of his indebtedness and his account, therefore, should show no balance. If, on the other hand, $500 is the amount recorded in the cash entry, the cash account at the end of the month will be overstated by $10. To meet these objections, two entries are required, one in

the Cash Receipts journal to show the actual amount of cash received, and one in the general journal to show the sales discount allowed. The entries are: 

19Oct. 15 

H. Dean CASH RECEIPTS 

Check for inv. 10/5 less 2% 490 00 JOURNAL 

Oct. 15, 19Discount on Sales

H. Dean 

Cash discount of 2% allowed for 10 day settlement of invoice of $500 of Oct. 5 1000 1000 TIT 

Special Discount Column in the Cash Book 

The foregoing method of entering a cash receipt involving a sales discount may be criticized on the ground that the record is made in two books of entry, giving in neither book the complete history of the transaction and resulting in two credits to the customer's account for the settlement where one should suffice. 

By introducing into the cash book a special column for recording the cash discount on the sale, the entire record can be shown in the cash book and a single credit posting can be made to the customer's account. The following form illustrates the complete entry in the cash book through the use of a special discount column.

Date F Account, Cr. 

19Oct. 15 H. Dean CASH RECEIPTS Explanation Account 

Cr. Discount on Sales

Net Cash 

For inv. 10/5 less 2% 500 00 1000 490 00

MERCHANDISE DISCOUNT 

301 The entry shows three amounts, the first a credit to H. 

Dean's account, the second a debit to Discount on Sales, and the third a debit to Cash. The posting to H. Dean's account should be made promptly from the entry in the cash book, so that his account may be kept up to date. The postings to the 

Discount on Sales and the Cash accounts are not made until the end of the month when both of these accounts are debited with the totals shown in their corresponding columns in the cash book. The Discount on Sales column serves two purposes. First, it reconciles the amount of actual cash received with the amount credited to the customer, and secondly, it serves as a labor saving device by reducing all the individual debits to the Sales Discount account to a single posting at the end of the month. The following model illustrates the summary entry for posting after the cash receipts book has been closed for the month.

Closing the Discount on Sales Account 

As the Discount on Sales account shows the decrease in capital resulting from a settlement of an account receivable by a receipt of cash of smaller amount, it is necessary when the books are closed to transfer this loss to the Profit and Loss account, as shown in the following accounts, by a closing journal entry, debiting Profit and Loss and crediting Discount on Sales.

DISCOUNT ON SALES

PROFIT AND LOSS 19Dec. 31 Sales Disct. J 6 155 65 

Rules for Debiting and Crediting the Discount on Sales Account DISCOUNT ON SALES 

Debit Discount on Sales for the decrease in capital resulting from sales discounts allowed on accounts receivable. 

Credit the Discount on Sales account to close it into the Profit and Loss account.

Summary 

1. Trade discounts are deducted from the list prices of articles to obtain the invoice prices.

MERCHANDISE DISCOUNT 

303 

2. Trade discounts serve (1) to effect changes in prices of articles listed in the manufacturer's or the distributor's catalogue, and (2) to keep from customers the dealer's cost price of articles selected from the catalogue. 

3. Cash discounts are parts of the terms of sale and are deducted from the total amount of the invoice if the latter is paid within the cash discount period. 

4. Cash discounts serve (1) to reduce the problem of collecting accounts by encouraging prompt payment, and (2) to provide the seller with working funds before the customer's full credit period has elapsed. 

5. Trade discounts are not made part of the bookkeeping record. 

6. Cash discounts are entered by the seller in a special "discount" column in his Cash Receipts journal and posted in total in the Discount on Sales account at the end of the month. 

7. At the periodic closing of the books, the temporary capital account Discount on Sales is closed by transferring the balance to the Profit and Loss account.

2. What is gained by publishing the trade discounts on catalogue articles on a separate discount sheet?

If you were able to borrow at your bank at the rate of 6 per cent per annum, would you still have the same preference? Why?

5. Show how the "Discount on Sales" column in the Cash Receipts journal serves two distinct purposes. 

6. Classify the Discount on Sales account and indicate how it is treated when the ledger is closed.

44. MERCHANDISE DISCOUNT (Continued) 

Discount on Purchases

We shall now consider the nature and treatment of cash discounts allowed on credit accounts from the standpoint of the buyer. If, for example, he owes $1000 on account for goods purchased on the terms: 2/10, n/30, and pays $980 within 10 days after the date of purchase, he cancels a debt of $1000 with $980 cash, thereby saving $20. Analyzing this in terms of assets, liabilities, and capital, we find that he has decreased his assets by $980 in order to decrease his liabilities by $1000, resulting in an increase in capital of $20. The account in which this capital increase is recorded is called the Discount on Purchases account.

Entries Involving Discount on Purchases In its simplest journal form the double entry record for a cash payment on account less a cash discount is: Creditor Cash $1000 $980 20 

Discount on Purchases

In order that the complete entry involving these three accounts may be shown in the Cash Payments journal, a special column is introduced for purchase discounts. The creditor's account is debited for the full amount of the liability canceled at the time the entry is made. The Cash and the 

Discount on Purchases accounts are credited in lump sums at the end of the month. The model on the next page illustrates the three-column Cash Payments journal and the method of summarizing and posting at the end of the month. The special column for purchase discounts does away with the necessity of making an additional entry in the general journal for the discount when a remittance to a creditor is recorded. It also makes it possible to record in the Cash Payments journal the full amount of the debt canceled and the net amount of cash paid. 306

4. Enter the totals in ink and double rule all the money columns except the two net cash columns. 

5. Bring down on the receipt side the balance at the beginning of the month, enter the amount in the net cash column, and add it to the total net receipts.

6. Subtract the total net payments from the total obtained in step 5, and enter the difference as the balance on the payment side. Some bookkeepers prefer to write this balance in red ink. 

7. Add the total payments and the balance on hand at the end of the month. The two net cash columns should now add up to the same amount. Double rule the two net cash columns. 

8. Write the summary entry for each side. These summary entries are sometimes made in the general journal. 

9. Bring down on the receipt side the balance on hand obtained in step 6, entering it as of the beginning of the next period. This balance is written "short."

Summary 

1. Cash discounts are entered by the buyer in a special column headed "Discount on Purchases" in the Cash Payments journal. 

2. At the end of the month when the cash book is summarized, the total of this column is credited in a temporary capital account called Discount on Purchases. 

3. At the periodic closing of the ledger the Discount on 

Purchases account is closed into the Profit and Loss account by transfer entry.

45. INTEREST DISCOUNT 

Although interest on notes becomes payable when the notes fall due, an exception to the rule is made in note transactions with banks. Two common types of such transactions will be considered, one involving the giving of a note for a cash loan made by the bank, and the other involving the sale of a note receivable to the bank. 

Case 1-Discounting a Note Payable 

Let us assume that you apply at your bank for a loan of $1000 for two months. Your banker will require you to submit your balance sheet so that he may pass on your financial condition. If it is found satisfactory, he will take your note for $1000 for two months and, in exchange, will credit your account with this sum less the interest for the period of the loan. If the interest rate is 6 per cent, your bank balance will be increased by $990, which is the face of the note less $10 interest for two months. In this way the banker is compensated at the time the loan is made instead of being obliged to wait for the interest payment until the loan falls due. The practice of deducting the interest in advance works to the banker's advantage in two ways. 

First, he actually receives a return slightly over 6 per cent on the loan, for he earns $10 on an outlay of $990 instead of $10 on $1000, the face of the note. Secondly, he has the use of $10 two months longer than he would have if he advanced the full amount of the loan and collected the interest at maturity. 

The sum deducted by the bank from the amount due at maturity is called the Bank Discount. The sum advanced by the bank, which is the difference between the amount due at maturity and the bank discount is called the Proceeds of the note. The note is usually made payable to the order of the maker and is indorsed by him to the bank, as is illustrated on the next page. 315

Reading, Pa., June 7, 19No. 84 

Three months after date I promise to pay to the order of myself 

Five Hundred 00/100 

Value received. $ 500.00

George Reigart 

Dollars 

The indorsement will read: 

Pay to the order of

The Pioneer State Bank George Reigart Analysis and Records of the Transaction 

In the negotiation of this loan at the bank, the borrower's asset Cash is increased by $990 and his liability Notes Payable is increased by $1000. The difference, $10, represents the service charge for the use of the bank's money and is charged to the Interest Cost account. As this service extends over a period of two months, the interest cost item represents an asset at the time the loan is made, which diminishes gradually up to the point of complete consumption when maturity is reached. Thus, the 

Interest Cost account, as was observed earlier in the chapter, should be classified as an asset account, since the interest charge represents a money service to be rendered during the life of the note.

The effects on assets and liabilities noted above are expressed in the journal entry, Cash 

Interest Cost 

$990 10 $1000 

Notes Payable 

As this is a cash transaction, it is necessary to frame this entry in the cash receipts journal. If a two-column cash jour-

1000 Bank discount on loan of $1000 made by bank for 2 months at 6 per cent interest (Cash Book . .)

As was observed in the treatment of sales discounts, the objection to this treatment is that the complete record does not appear in one place and that the total indebtedness is not shown in the Notes Payable account in one sum. The three-column cash book meets this difficulty. The whole record for the transaction appears as follows:

One column is used for sales discounts and interest discounts, as the latter in the average business house do not occur often enough to warrant the use of a separate column. It is necessary to distinguish between these two types of discount so that when the column is footed the correct totals may be readily

obtained for the Discount on Sales and the Interest Cost accounts. Bookkeepers use various devices for marking the interest items in the discount column, such as encircling the amount, writing in red ink, or inserting the letter "I" as shown in the illustration. Case 2 Discounting Notes Receivable 

Notes are made negotiable by the insertion of the phrase "to the order of." This enables the holder of a note to offer it in payment to another party as a substitute for cash, or to convert it into cash by discounting it at the bank if he wishes to dispose of it before maturity. 

Let us assume that you hold Samuel Tilden's note for $800, dated May 5 and due in 4 months, and that on Aug. 6 you discount the note at your bank at the rate of 6 per cent. 

As far as the bank is concerned, this is equivalent to discounting your own note for $800 on Aug. 6 to mature on 

Sept. 5, the due date of Tilden's note. Your indorsement on the note will render you liable to the bank should Tilden fail to pay.

The bank discount on Tilden's note is calculated on the maturity value of the note for the discount period of 30 days

INTEREST DISCOUNT 

319 from Aug. 6 to Sept. 5. It should be noted that the maturity value and not the face value of the note is taken as the basis for this calculation. In the case of a non-interest-bearing note, both values are identical. In the case of an interest-bearing note, however, the maturity value is the sum of the face value and the interest for the life of the note. This gives rise to two distinct types of bank discount transactions, one involving the non-interest-bearing note and the other dealing with the interestbearing note. 

Discounting a Non-interest-bearing Note

Assuming Tilden's note to be non-interest-bearing, the value at maturity will be $800. The bank discount will be 6 per cent of this sum for 30 days, or $4. The proceeds, therefore, will be $796. These facts are represented in the following diagram. 

Face Value 

$800 May 5 

Date of Note 

Value

Time 

Your cash record for the transaction is Proceeds $796 

Maturity Value 

$800 1 

Terms of Discount 

Aug. 6

Date of Discount 

Sept. 5

Date of Maturity CASH RECEIPTS Accounts 

Cr.

Discount on Sales

Net Cash 19Aug. 

6 Notes Receiv- Disct. Tilden's note able of 5/5 for 30 da. 800 00 I 400 796 00 This record when posted will cancel the note receivable on ⚫your books. When the cash book is summarized, Cash will be debited for the net amount received, and Interest Cost will be debited for the cost of obtaining this money before maturity.

When the note falls due it will be presented by the bank to Tilden for payment. This will involve no further record on your books. 

Discounting an Interest-bearing Note

Let us assume that the note you received from S. Tilden bears interest at the rate of 6 per cent per annum. If you indorsed this note on Aug. 6 to F. Baker, a creditor, as a payment on account, you would be entitled to a credit of $812.40, which is the face of the note, $800, plus 93 days' interest earned from 

May 5 to Aug. 6 at the rate of 6 per cent. Your journal entry for the transaction would be 

F. Baker

Notes Receivable 

$812.40 $800.00 12.40 

Interest Income 

Indorsed Tilden's note of May 6 to F. Baker to apply on account, receiving credit for the face and interest at 6 per cent for 93 days. 

If, instead of transferring the note to a creditor on Aug. 6, you decided to discount it at your bank, you would receive a cash sum equal to the maturity value of the note, $816 (the face and 4 months' interest), less the bank discount on $816 for 30 days, the term of discount from Aug. 6 to Sept. 5. The proceeds would, therefore, be $816 less the bank discount of $4.08, or $811.92.

There are two reasons why banks use this method of finding the cash value of the note instead of adding to the face value the interest accumulated from the date of the note as was done in indorsing the note to F. Baker. First, it is desirable that the proceeds shall be uniformly calculated on the maturity value, whether the note be non-interest or interest bearing. Secondly, the bank operates on the theory that in discounting your note it is advancing the funds due you at maturity and is entitled to the interest on this amount for the period over which this advance is made.

Prepayment of Notes Payable 

The payment of a note by the maker before maturity is known as prepayment. The object in prepaying is either to accommodate the holder who may be in need of funds and cannot conveniently discount the note, or to cancel the debt for a smaller cash sum. 

Thus, if a note is issued by a firm June 10 for $500 for 4 months and is prepaid Aug. 10, the amount needed to cancel the indebtedness is $495, which is the maturity value of the note less the discount at 6 per cent for the unexpired 2 months. The maker's cash book entry for the prepayment is,

CASH PAYMENTS Accounts 

Dr. 

Disc. on

Purchases 

Net Cash 19Aug. 6 Notes Payable Prepaid my note 500 00 I 5 00495 00 of 6/10 favor of J. Dunn 

The interest discount is entered in the "Discount on Purchases" column and is earmarked I, so that when the column is totaled at the end of the month, the interest items may be distinguished from the purchase discount items. The summary entry in closing the Cash Payments journal is,

Aug. 31. V Sundry Accounts, Dr. 

Cash, Cr. 

Discount on Purchases, Cr. $.... $... 

Interest Income, Cr. • .... $.

Summary 

1. When applying for a loan at the bank, the borrower gives his personal note. The bank deducts the bank discount, which is the interest on the note, and credits the borrower's account with the proceeds. 

2. The entry for the discounting of the borrower's note is made in the Cash Receipts journal, the bank discount being inserted in the Discount on Sales column and earmarked to distinguish it from the sales discount items. 

3. A depositor may have his notes receivable discounted at his bank. The bank discount is computed on the maturity value of the note, which is the same as the face value in the case of a non-interest bearing note, or the face value plus the interest in the case of an interest bearing note.

INTEREST DISCOUNT 

323 4. In prepaying a note, the interest discount is computed on the maturity value. If the sum paid by the maker is less than the face value the difference represents interest income. 

In his entry for the prepayment in the Cash Payments journal, the interest discount is recorded in the Discount on Purchases column and earmarked to distinguish it from the purchase discount items.

46.  ON THE  Classifications in the Statement of Profit and Loss 

The question arises as to how to treat the interest items and the sales discount and the purchase discount items on the statement of profit and loss. It has been shown that this statement is made up of two sections, the trading section, in which the sales income and the sundry items of cost of merchandise are assembled to determine the gross profit on sales, and the operating expense section, which sets forth all items of expense incurred in operating the business. The difference between the gross profit on sales and the total operating expense represents the net profit or loss from operating the business. This net profit or loss measures the success or failure of the business.

Treatment of Interest Income and Interest Cost 

Interest Income is realized by extending credit to customers on their notes and overdue accounts and by lending cash or keeping it on deposit in the bank. Clearly, such income is in addition to the regular profits realized in buying and selling merchandise, and should therefore be shown on the statement of profit and loss. For this purpose it is necessary to provide a third section in addition to the two sections noted above. This third section is headed "Operating Income" and contains all items of income derived from operation of the business other than income from sales. 

Interest Cost represents the charge for credit extended on notes or accounts by creditors or for money borrowed. 

When this credit service or money service has been consumed, an expense is incurred in financing the business. This ex- 328

ON THE PROFIT AND LOSS STATEMENT 

329 pense is reported in the "Operating Expenses" section on the statement of profit and loss.

Treatment of Discounts on Purchases and on Sales 

There has been much discussion among accountants as to whether the cash discounts allowed on merchandise should be treated as trading items and be deducted directly from purchases and sales, or whether they should be reported as operating income and operating expense items respectively on the statement of profit and loss. Those who hold the first view regard the amount actually expended as the true cost of the goods and the amount actually received as the true sales price of the goods, the difference being the profit realized in trading. Thus, if merchandise is bought for $100, terms: 

2/10, n/30, the actual cost is only $98 if payment is made within 10 days. Likewise, if the merchandise is sold for $125, terms: 2/10, n/30, the actual return is only $122.50, if the customer pays within 10 days, and the net profit on the sale. is $24.50 ($122.50 less $98).

Those who treat discounts on purchases and on sales as trading items of income and expense maintain that the invoice prices at which the merchandise is bought and sold represent the true cost and income in trading, and that the difference between the two is the trading profit. Furthermore, the discount on purchases is compensation derived by giving creditors cash before it is due, and the discount on sales is the cost of obtaining cash from customers before their accounts are due. Thus, in the example given in the preceding paragraph, the profit on the sale according to this view, is $125 less $100, or $25. The purchase discount of $2 represents an additional operating income and the sales discount of $2.50 represents an operating expense involved in financing the business.

The two views for the treatment of sales and purchase discounts on the statement of profit and loss are reflected in

the following tabulations: 

CASH DISCOUNTS AS TRADING ITEMS CASH DISCOUNTS AS OPERATING ITEMS 

Sale $125.00 

Less Discount on Sale 2.50 Sale

Purchase 

$125.00 100.00 

Net Sale 

$122.50 

Profit on Sale $ 25.00

Operating Income Purchase 

$100.00

Discount on Purchase 

2.00 

Less Discount on Pur 

Total Income 

$ 27.00 chase 2.00 

Operating Expense Net Purchase 

98.00 'Discount on Sale 2.50 

Profit on Sale $ 24.50

Net Income 

$ 24.50

Merchants frequently borrow at the bank in order to raise funds for discounting their bills. It seems logical that if the expense involved in the bank discount deducted by the bank is treated as an operating expense, the purchase discount thereby saved should be treated correspondingly as operating income. Another means of obtaining funds is by offering sales discounts to customers. It seems both reasonable and consistent that the expense involved should be treated as in the case of interest cost, as an item of operating expense. The present tendency is in favor of the method of treating cash discounts on purchases and on sales as items of operating income and operating expense. This method will be followed in this text and is illustrated in the statement of profit and loss on the next page.

This statement contains three sections: (1) the trading section in which the gross profit on sales is established, (2) the operating expenses section which shows the total cost of supplies and services consumed in selling, managing, and financing the business, and (3) the operating income section, which shows all earnings or income realized from operations in addition to the regular profits made on sales. It should be noted that the total operating income is deducted from the total operating expenses to obtain the net operating expense, and that the last in turn is deducted from the gross profit on sales in the trading section to obtain the net profit for the period.

Total Operating Income Net Profit for the Period Summary 

1. Interest service is consumed when the interest period has expired. The interest expense incurred is reported as an operating expense on the statement of profit and loss. 

2. Income from sources other than sales is shown in a third section called operating income on the statement of profit and loss. Interest income is one of the items in this group. 

3. Discounts on sales and on purchases are operating items. The total discount on sales is shown as operating expense, and the total discount on purchases as operating income on the statement of profit and loss.

4. The total operating expense less the total operating income gives the net operating expense. The gross trading profit less the net operating expense gives the net profit for the period.

CHAPTER XI SPECIAL FEATURES IN  49. MERCHANDISE RETURNS AND ALLOWANCES Specialization in the Journals 

It has been shown that the special journals, such as the cash book, the purchase book and the sales book, are outgrowths of the general journal to meet the need for labor-saving methods in making the bookkeeping records. The application of the principle of the special journal will be extended in this chapter to embrace returns and allowances made on sales and on purchases.

Sales returns and purchase returns occur so frequently in wholesale mercantile establishments, that it is found economical to enter these returns in special journals in place of the general journal. The labor-saving features observed in the study of the sales, the purchase, and the cash journals are present here, as will be apparent from a study of the model return books shown below and at the foot of page 345.

Comments on the Sales Return and Allowance Journal 

When a customer receives merchandise that is unsatisfactory, he either returns it or submits a claim for an allowance on the cost price. Allowances may be asked also for shortage, breakage, spoilage, and the like. Finally, there may be an error in price or calculation on the invoice calling for an adjustment. In any of these cases, a credit memorandum is sent by the seller to the customer as soon as the goods are returned or the claim is granted. A duplicate copy of the credit memorandum is usually returned by the seller, from which the record of the return or the allowance is made in the Sales Return and Allowance Book. The customer's account is immediately credited with the amount involved. At the end of the month one debit entry is made for the total sales returns and allowances for the month.

The Sales Returns and Allowances Account 

Instead of debiting the Sales account with the total sales returns and allowances, a special account headed Sales Returns and Allowances may be used. This separation from the sales record is very desirable, because it draws attention to the matter of customers' returns and complaints and warns the management of any serious faults inherent in the merchandise or arising from the methods employed in packing, shipping, and billing the goods.

The Sales Returns and Allowances account is closed into the Sales Income account, as shown here, when the ledger is closed

MERCHANDISE RETURNS AND ALLOWANCES 

345 It is sometimes maintained that sales returns and sales allowances should be recorded in two separate accounts, because they are fundamentally different in nature. A sales return, it is claimed, is merely a reduction of the original sale, as the merchandise is actually returned, whereas a sales allowance represents an actual reduction of the profit originally realized on the sale. Where this distinction is made by using two accounts, it is necessary to have two amount columns in the Sales Returns and Allowances journal, one for returns and one for allowances, so that separate debit totals for the Sales Returns account and the Sales Allowances account may be readily obtained when this special journal is summarized. 

Purchase Returns and Allowances

The treatment of purchase returns and allowances in the special return and allowance journal follows the plan outlined for sales returns and allowances. Here again a saving is effected by debiting each creditor as the return or allowance is entered in the Purchase Return and Allowance Book and by crediting the Purchase Returns and Allowances account at the end of the month for the total amount entered. 

The following forms illustrate the Purchase Return and 

Allowance Book and the closing of the return account by transfer to the Merchandise Purchases account when the ledger is closed.

Returns and Allowances on the Profit and Loss Statement

The treatment here conforms to that in the ledger closing. 

The sales returns and allowances are shown as direct deductions from the gross sales income, leaving a net sales income. Similarly, the purchase returns and allowances are deducted from purchases, leaving the net purchases. The trading section of the statement of profit and loss is presented here to illustrate the treatment of returns and allowances.

MERCHANDISE RETURNS AND ALLOWANCES 347 Summary 

1. Special Return and Allowance journals may be used to advantage where there are many returns and allowances on purchases and on sales. 

2. Entries are made in these special journals for goods returned, for damaged or defective goods, for breakage, for spoilage, for shortage, for overcharge, etc. 

3. Returns from or allowances to customers are credited immediately in the customers' accounts, and the total for the month, when the Sales Return and Allowance journal is summarized, is debited in the Sales Returns and Allowances account. 

4. The balance of the Sales Returns and Allowances account is transferred to the Sales Income account when the ledger is closed. 

5. Returns to or allowances from creditors are debited immediately in the creditors' accounts, and the total for the month, when the Purchase Return and Allowance journal is summarized, is credited in the Purchase Returns and Allowances account. 

6. The balance of the Purchase Returns and Allowances account is transferred to the Merchandise Purchases account when the ledger is closed. 

7. On the statement of profit and loss, sales returns and allowances are deducted from sales income, and purchase returns and allowances are deducted from purchases. 

1.

Show how the returns and allowances journals are labor-saving devices. 

2. Distinguish between a sales return and a sales allowance. How does each affect assets, liabilities, and capital? 

3. Distinguish between a purchase return and a purchase allowance. How does each affect assets, liabilities, and capital?

4. Indicate the treatment of returns and allowances (a) in preparing a statement of profit and loss, (b) in closing the ledger. 

5. In looking through his ledger, a merchant notes that his gross sales for the past two months are close to $15,000 and that the returns and allowances against these sales approximately amount to $1,500. 

The sales returns and allowances for the year previous to this period average 3 per cent of the sales. Compare the rate of sales returns and allowances for the past two months with the average for the previous year. How should the merchant proceed to find the causes of the present situation? What should he do to remedy the condition?

50.  

The principle of specialization of the journal is carried out in two ways, one through the use of the special journal, and the other through the introduction of special money columns in the journals. The discount columns and the net cash columns in the cash book are illustrations of the special column as a labor-saving device. 

There is no limit as to the number and kinds of special columns that may be employed in the journals. Each set of journals is designed to meet the practical needs of a particular business. 

That is one reason why accountants are called in to install bookkeeping systems. No attempt can be made in an elementary text to present some of the detailed journal forms that have been evolved. A few illustrations of the special column in the journal are presented here to show its purpose and use as a bookkeeping device.

Special Columns in the Cash Book 

One of the special columns frequently used on the receipt side of the cash book is the Cash Sales column, in which all sales

Special Columns in the Purchase and the Sales Journals 

In addition to its value as a labor-saving device, the special column is used for other purposes. The student will become

acquainted with these in his study of advanced bookkeeping. 

One of these purposes, however, will now be considered. 

Many trading establishments carry two or three distinct lines of merchandise and operate a separate department for each line; for example, one house may specialize in household furniture and carpets, another in woolens and silks, still another in electrical supplies and radio supplies, etc. In any of these instances, the merchant is not satisfied with knowing merely the net gain from operating the business as a whole, but wishes to know to what extent each department has contributed to the net result. 

If the trading records of these departments are all merged in one set of accounts, the loss of one department may be covered and concealed by the profits of another department. It is highly important, therefore, that the showing of each department shall be readily ascertained from the book records. To supply such information it is necessary to keep separate accounts for the inventories, purchases and sales of each department. 

The data for these accounts can be most conveniently obtained by means of special columns in both the purchase and the sales journals.

It is not the intention to introduce at this point a study of any system of departmental bookkeeping. The departmental type of business is mentioned merely to illustrate a definite use that is made of the special column in the purchase and in the sales journals. The models presented here indicate first, how by the use of special columns the purchases and the sales are segregated by departments and secondly, how the departmental information is carried to the ledger by means of the summary entries.

1. There are two forms of specialization of the journal, (1) through the use of special journals, and (2) through the use of special money columns in the various journals. 

2. The special column is a labor-saving device, as the items in it are posted in a lump sum at the end of the month to the proper account. 

3. Special columns are used for items that appear frequently in the journal. Examples are the "cash sales" column and various supply and service columns in the cash book. 

4. Each entry appearing in a special column is checked to prevent individual posting of the entry. 

5. Special columns may be used in the purchase and sales journals for the purpose of keeping separate merchandise and sales records of various departments of the establishment. 

1. Discuss two uses of special columns in books of original entry, and illustrate each.

2. A retailer makes on the average 150 cash sales a day, the average sale amounting to $1.35. Suggest an economical plan of entering and posting his cash sales. 

3. Show how each of the following special columns in the cash book is a labor-saving device: Net Cash, Discount on Sales, Store 

Supplies and Services, Cash Sales.

Assume that the books have just been closed for the year and that Mr. Crosby wishes to modify his bookkeeping system so that the results accomplished in each department will be known. Present a complete plan for carrying out Mr. Crosby's wish.

CHAPTER XII 

DRAFTS 

52.  

The draft originated among merchants in ancient times as an instrument for settling their debts when communication was very poor and slow, and when the shipment of money was subject to many hazards. There were no banking facilities or other commercial agencies through which the transfer of funds to distant points could be made.

How the practice of settling accounts by draft developed can best be understood by considering an example close at hand. 

If you owed Smith $100 and Jones owed you $100, you could instruct Jones to pay $100 directly to Smith, so that both Jones' debt to you and your debt to Smith would be canceled. This would be particularly desirable if Smith and Jones were situated remotely from you and near each other, because the transfer of funds between them could be made more quickly and safely than by transmitting the money from Jones to you and back again from you to Smith. The short-cut for settling the two debts is illustrated in the following diagram: 

Student (New York) 

Owes $100 to 

Owes $100 to 

Jones (Chicago) Pays $100 to 

Smith (Chicago) 365

DRAFTS 

To accomplish this exchange, you would draw up a formal order on Jones and would send it to Smith, who, in turn, would present it to Jones and receive payment. Such an order is a draft, which may be defined as a written request or order by one party on another to pay a fixed sum of money to the order of a third party. The party drawing the draft is called the Drawer. The one that is directed to make the payment is called the Drawee. The party designated as the one to receive the money is called the Payee. The draft may be made payable either at sight, that is, when presented to the payee, or at some future time. A draft payable at sight is known as a sight draft; a draft payable at some future time is known as a time draft. This section deals with the sight draft. The time draft and its practical applications are discussed in section 54.

The form of a three-party sight draft is presented in the following illustration: 

No.126 

New York, May 4, 19Absight 

AugaL Butter the order of Hugo Le 00 

Five Hundred and we 100 Payte $.500

Dollars Value received and charge the same to account of 

To Thomas Halls & Francis Miller 

275 State St. (Chicago, Ill

This draft is drawn by Francis Miller and sent to Hugo L. 

Butler, who presents it to Thomas Wells for payment. As the draft is negotiable, Butler may either present it himself for payment or indorse it to another party. 

The use of the three-party draft in modern practice for the purpose of clearing two debts among the parties named is quite rare. Any detailed reference to this type of draft could hardly be justified, were it not for the fact that the three-party draft embodies the bookkeeping principles underlying other forms of

commercial drafts in common use. For this reason, it is desirable to consider the bookkeeping aspect of this type of draft. 

Entries of the Parties to a Three-Party Sight Draft 

At the time the draft is drawn, the drawer holds a claim against the drawee, and the payee holds a claim against the drawer. No entry is made by the drawer when he writes the draft as there is no assurance that the drawee will honor (i. e.pay) it. For the same reason, the payee makes no entry on receipt of the draft. When the drawee pays the draft, the drawer's claim against him is met, as is also that of the payee against the drawer for an equal sum. It is necessary to show the discharge of indebtedness on the books of each of the parties. 

(a) The Drawer's Entry 

Referring to the draft illustrated on page 297, we note that Francis Miller, the drawer, settles his indebtedness of $500 to Hugo L. Butler and at the same time surrenders his claim of $500 against Thomas Wells when the latter pays the draft. 

To show the decrease in his liability Accounts Payable and the decrease in his asset Accounts Receivable, Miller makes the following entry in his journal: 

Hugo L. Butler 

Thomas Wells 

May 6, 19H. L. Butler met sight draft drawn on him in favor of T. Wells 500 00 500 00 

(b) The Drawee's Entry 

Thomas Wells by paying the draft settles his indebtedness of $500 to F. Miller. To show the decrease in his liability 

Accounts Payable and the decrease in Cash, he makes the following cash book record:

CASH PAYMENTS 

19May 

6 Francis Miller Paid his S/D favor of Butler for invoice 4/5 500 00 500 00

DRAFTS (c) The Payee's Entry 

On receipt of the money, Hugo L. Butler surrenders his claim against F. Miller for the amount received, thereby increasing his cash and decreasing his accounts receivable. This is reflected in his cash book record. 19CASH RECEIPTS 

May 6 Francis Miller S/D on T. Wells to apply on account 500 00 500 00 

The Two-Party Sight Draft 

The two-party sight draft is a variation of the three-party draft in which the drawer and the payee represent the same individual or concern; that is, the drawer makes the draft payable to himself or his order, as is illustrated in the following draft: 

TWO-PARTY SIGHT DRAFT 

No_84 — Kingston, NY Aug 10, 19the order of Atsight

Myself 00 

Live Hundred and s Payto $5000

Dollars Value received and charge the same to account of 

To Wallace Burdick Lidney Trent 

Buffalo, NY.

The two-party sight draft is frequently employed as a means of collecting current or overdue accounts. This is accomplished in the following way. The drawer indorses the draft to his bank, instructing it to make the collection. The drawer's bank, in turn, indorses and forwards the draft to its correspondent, which is some bank in the city where the drawee is located. 

The correspondent bank presents the draft to the drawee. The latter is not legally obliged to honor the draft, but will probably meet it so that his credit and business reputation with the local

bankers may not be impaired. The correspondent bank remits the funds collected to the drawer's bank, where the drawer's account is credited for the sum received less the collection charge made by the correspondent. The procedure for making a collection by sight draft is illustrated in the following diagram: Drawer Kingston Bank 

Course of the Draft 

Drawee Buffalo Bank 

Course of the Remittance Entries for a Two-Party Sight Draft

C 43 50 In closing the ledger an adjustment entry is made debiting Profit and Loss and crediting Collection and Exchange. The collection charges are reported under Operating Expenses on the statement of profit and loss. 

Summary 

1. In a three party sight draft, the drawer orders the drawee to pay a fixed sum of money at sight to the payee or to his order. 

2. When a sight draft is paid each party to the instrument makes an entry on his books. The drawer makes a journal entry debiting the payee's account and crediting the drawee's account. The drawee makes an entry in the Cash 

Payments journal, debiting the drawer's account and credit-

ing Cash. The payee makes an entry in the Cash Receipts journal, debiting Cash and crediting the drawer's account. 

3. In the two-party sight draft the drawer makes the instrument payable to himself or his order. This type of draft is used as a means of collecting overdue accounts through the drawer's bank. 

4. When a two party sight draft is paid, the drawer makes two entries, one in the Cash Receipts journal, crediting the drawee's account for the amount of the draft and the other in the Cash Payments journal, debiting Collection and Exchange for the collection charge deducted by the bank. 

The drawee's entry is made in the Cash Payments journal, debiting the drawer's account.

53. APPLICATION OF  Special Forms of the Three-Party Sight Draft 

In settling their accounts with one another, business men find it inconvenient to pass money in considerable amounts from hand to hand and unsafe to transmit it in the mail. Several substitutes for money have been devised which business men generally accept in payment of debts. All of these equivalents embody the principle of the three-party sight draft. Those most commonly used are the check, the money order, and the bank draft. 

The Check 

The most common method of making payment that does not involve the actual use of currency is by check. Practically every man of affairs maintains a checking account with a bank, and many people in private life prefer to make their disbursements by check rather than in currency. A check is in reality a sight draft, for it is an order by a depositor on his bank to pay out of funds he has deposited a sum of money on demand to bearer or to the order of a third party named in the check. The depositor is the drawer of the instrument, the bank is the drawee and the one to whom the check is made payable is the payee. 

The relationship of creditor and debtor between the drawer and the drawee in a sight draft exists between the depositor and his bank. In most cases, also, the payee is a creditor of the drawer of the check.

The Money Order 

When a person who has no checking account wishes to make a payment with an equivalent for money, he may exchange his currency for either a postal money order at any post office or an express money order at the office of an express company. If he decides to obtain a postal money order, he must fill out an application (either domestic or foreign) at the post office and pay in currency to the postmaster the amount of the money order plus a small service fee. The money order he receives is 374

APPLICATION OF  

375 drawn on the postmaster in the place where the one to whom the money order is to be remitted is located. The payee named in the money order may cash it at his local post office upon proper identification, deposit it at his bank, or negotiate it by indorsement to another party. Only one such negotiation is permitted. 15 20 25 30 90057

A similar procedure is followed in purchasing and redeeming an express money order. The sender buys the money order from the agent at the local office of the express company. Usually the purchaser is named as the payee in the instrument. He indorses it and forwards it to the one to whom he wishes to make payment. The latter may cash it at any branch office of the company, deposit it at his bank or negotiate it further. It is readily seen that the essential elements of the three-party sight draft are present in both kinds of money order.

DRAFTS The Bank Draft 

Another form of the three-party sight draft used as an equivalent for money in making remittances is the bank draft, which may be defined as an order by one bank on another to pay a fixed sum of money to the order of the party named in the draft. This instrument is sometimes referred to as a bill of exchange. The bank draft is frequently used for obtaining merchandise promptly from out-of-town concerns with whom the purchaser has not established credit. For example, John 

Dillon of New York orders goods for cash of C. Halley & Co., Atlanta, Ga. Dillon has done no previous business with this concern and is in urgent need of the merchandise. If he sends his personal check with the order, the shipment of the goods will be delayed for several days until Halley & Co. are certain that Dillon's check has gone through and that there are sufficient funds in Dillon's bank account to meet it. On the other hand, if Dillon incloses a bank draft, it will be accepted in payment without question and the goods will be shipped promptly. 

A depositor at a bank finds it more convenient to remit by bank draft than by money order. To obtain a bank draft he draws his personal check on the bank for an amount covering the face of the draft and the cost of exchange. He indicates thereon the place in which the drawee bank is situated. Thus, if he wishes to remit to a party in Atlanta, he makes his check payable to Atlanta Draft. In exchange for his check, the depositor receives a bank draft made out directly to his order. 

This is preferable to having the draft made out directly to the one to whom it is sent, as it enables the latter to know who the sender is, and serves as evidence of payment in the event of any dispute. Before forwarding the bank draft, the sender indorses it to the party to whom he remits. The latter may deposit it at his own bank, or on proper identification, cash it at the bank on which it is drawn. 

Both the bank draft and the check with which it is purchased are illustrated on page 377. John C. Dillon gives his check for $500.50 to the National City Bank of New York and receives in return a bank draft on the Merchants National Bank of Atlanta. 

Using the example cited above, he will indorse the draft "Pay to the order of C. Halley & Co., (signed) John C. Dillon."

No.C. 3501 Applications of the Two-Party Sight Draft 

The method of collecting current and overdue accounts by means of the two-party sight draft was presented in the preceding section. Another use frequently made of this form of draft is in connection with shipments by freight in which the terms of sale are C. O. D., i. e.-cash on delivery. To understand the procedure followed in such shipments, it is necessary to consider the general topic of shipping merchandise. 

Shipping by Freight 

When a merchant wishes to ship merchandise to an out-oftown point, he has four agencies at his command. He may make the shipment by parcel post, express, motor truck, or

DRAFTS 

freight. Goods put up in small parcels are usually shipped by parcel post or express. Goods packed in larger cases and goods in bulk are usually shipped by motor truck or freight. 

Freight transportation may be by railroad or steamship. Every freight shipment involves three parties (1) the railroad or steamship company, known as the carrier, (2) the one who turns the goods over to the carrier, known as the shipper, and (3) the one to whom the carrier delivers the goods at the place of arrival, known as the consignee. When a shipment is made by freight, the carrier issues to the shipper a Bill of Lading, which serves both as a receipt for the merchandise left with the transportation company and as a contract between the carrier and the shipper. In June, 1908, the Interstate Commerce Commission approved a uniform bill of lading designed to meet the requirements of the shipper and the carrier. There are two kinds of bills of lading, the 

Straight Bill of Lading and the Order Bill of Lading. The straight bill of lading is used in freight shipments in which the carrier is expected to deliver the goods to the consignee upon proper identification. The order bill of lading is used in freight shipments which must be paid for by the consignee before the goods are delivered to him by the transportation company.

Procedure in the Use of the Straight Bill of Lading 

To save time, the shipper usually prepares the bill of lading in advance, making three copies—an original copy, a shipping order, and a memorandum copy. He signs the original copy and the shipping order and turns the latter over with the goods to the freight agent at the freight yard or pier. The latter signs the original copy and the memorandum and returns them to the shipper, who retains the memorandum copy and mails the original copy to the customer. When the goods reach their destination, the freight agent at that end sends a notice of arrival to the customer, who, upon proper identification, receives the goods at the freight station. In case of any damage or loss while the goods are in transit, the original bill of lading must be produced in filing a claim against the carrier.

Procedure in the Use of the Order Bill of Lading 

A C. O. D.

shipment by freight differs essentially from a O.D. 

C. O. D. shipment by parcel post or by express in that the railroad or steamship company does not collect the payment from the consignee, whereas the post office department or the express company does make such collection before delivery and remits the proceeds to the shipper by money order. In the case of a 

C. O. D. shipment by freight, the collection and transmission of money from the customer to the shipper is accomplished through the shipper's bank. The procedure is as follows: 

The shipper makes out an Order Bill of Lading in triplicate.

This document is colored so as to be easily distinguishable from the straight bill of lading. Instead of being consigned directly to the customer as is the straight bill of lading, the document is made negotiable, being consigned to the order of the shipper.

DRAFTS 

When the goods are left with the carrier, the freight agent signs and returns to the shipper the original and the memorandum copies and retains the shipping order. The shipper then prepares a sight draft on the customer for the amount of the invoice, attaches it to the original bill of lading, and transfers both documents to his bank by indorsement. 381 turn indorses these papers and forwards them to its correspondent bank in the customer's city. The sight draft is presented to the drawee by the out-of-town bank, which on receipt of the money, indorses the order bill of lading to the customer. On surrender of this document to the local freight agent, the customer receives the goods. The correspondent bank remits the proceeds to the shipper's bank after deducting a fee for collection. The shipper's bank credits the shipper's account with the cash returns on the shipment. The course of the sight draft and the bill of lading attached, assuming that the shipper is located at New York and the buyer at Norfolk, is illustrated in the following diagram.

The C. O. D. Account 

C. O. D. sales must be charged to the customers' accounts so that the books will show the indebtedness until the cash returns on such shipments are received. There are two methods of keeping C. O. D. accounts. One is to open an account with each customer headed with his name and the terms C. O.D. to distinguish the account from the regular customers' accounts, as, for example, Charles Hepburn, (C. O. D.). The more common method is to group all C. O. D. charges in one account under the title of C. O. D. Accounts, as it is not probable that the sales to any one C. O. D. customer will occur often enough to warrant keeping a separate account with him. 

A composite C. O. D. account is shown on page 382. The customer's name is noted in the explanatory space when the account is charged with the amount of the sale. When the payment is received the account is credited, the credit amount being entered

DRAFTS on the same line as that on which the corresponding debit appears. This arrangement shows at a glance the unpaid items and thus helps to prevent tardy collections. 19June 5 T. Barry

Summary 

1. The check is the most common form used in business for making payments. Where ones' personal check is not acceptable, or one does not have a checking account, a money order (postal or express) or a bank draft may be used. 

2. Goods may be shipped to out-of-town points by parcel post, express, motor truck, or freight. When the last method is employed, a bill of lading is issued by the carrier. 

3. The straight bill of lading is used in ordinary freight shipments. The order bill of lading is used when the goods are shipped C.O.D. by freight. 

4. In a C.O.D. shipment by parcel post or express, the post office department or the express company collects the payment and remits it to the shipper. 

5. In a C.O.D. shipment by freight collection is made through the shipper's bank. The order bill of lading with a two-party sight draft attached is indorsed by the shipper to his bank. It forwards these papers to a correspondent which delivers the bill of lading to the consignee on receipt of payment, and remits the collection to the shipper's bank. The consignee receives the goods from the carrier on surrender of the bill of lading.

383 6. All C.O.D. sales are charged to the various customers in a composite C.O.D. account and the payments are credited in this account directly opposite to their corresponding charges.

54.  

A draft in which the drawee is directed to pay at some future time is known as a Time Draft. The maturity of a time draft is determined in one of two ways, by reckoning the time stated in the draft either from the date of the draft or from the date when it is seen by the drawee. For example, if a draft is dated 

June 10 and reads "Thirty days after date pay to the order of, etc.," it matures in 30 days from June 10, or on July 10. If the draft is dated June 10 and reads "Thirty days after sight," or, as is the more common wording, "At thirty days' sight," it falls due 30 days after it is presented to the drawee. Assuming that this is done on June 15, the draft matures on July 15. 

Acceptance of Time Drafts 

The time draft must be presented to the drawee twice, the first time to obtain his acceptance and the second time to obtain payment. The drawee accepts the draft by writing across the face of the instrument the word "Accepted" and his name underneath. By accepting, the drawee acknowledges the genuineness of the drawer's signature and agrees to make the payment at maturity. It is customary in writing the acceptance to include the date of acceptance. While this is not essential in a draft made payable after date, it is necessary in one made payable $ncom $250100

after sight for the purpose of determining the due date. After a time draft has been accepted, it is known as an Acceptance. 

A draft payable after date may be presented for payment at maturity even though it has not been previously presented for acceptance. A draft payable after sight cannot be presented for payment unless it has been accepted, as there is no way of fixing the date of maturity. The drawee is permitted twenty-four hours in which to accept or to return the draft without acceptance. 

If he fails to return the draft he is legally considered to have accepted and becomes liable on the instrument when it matures.. 

The time draft due after date is illustrated on page 386..

The following diagram shows the course taken by a twoparty time draft from the date of issue to the date of payment. 

The five steps are: (1) the forwarding of the draft from the drawer to the drawee for acceptance, (2) the return of the acceptance from the drawee to the drawer, (3) the transfer of the acceptance before maturity from the drawer to his bank for collection, (4) the forwarding of the acceptance by the drawer's bank to its correspondent in the drawee's city, and (5) the presentation of the acceptance by the correspondent to the drawee for payment.

By accepting the draft the drawee makes himself liable on a written instrument, thereby substituting a written agreement to pay the drawer for the oral or implied promise by which he has been bound. The effect of accepting is the same as if the debtor gave the creditor his promissory note. Acceptances, therefore, are equivalent to promissory notes and, for this reason, are recorded in the Notes Receivable account by the drawer of the two-party draft, and in the Notes Payable account by the drawee. Referring to the draft on page 387, the entries made by the two parties for the acceptance and the payment are as follows:

Another method is to credit Notes Receivable for the full amount in the Cash Receipts journal and to debit Collection and Exchange for the collection fee in the Cash Payments journal.

The Trade Acceptance 

An acceptance given directly for the purchase of merchandise is known as a Trade Acceptance. In form it is practically like any other acceptance with the added feature that it contains on its face a statement that the obligation of the acceptor arises out of the purchase of goods from the drawer.

BIONATURE OF DRAWER) By Theodore Whalen 

Treasu

The trade acceptance is gradually coming into favor with manufacturers and large distributors because it tends to do away with the customer's open account. There are several reasons why the trade acceptance makes a strong appeal to the seller. 

As soon as a sale subject to trade acceptance is completed, the seller may convert the acceptance into cash by discounting it at his bank, thereby acquiring ready capital at a slight cost of discount instead of being obliged either to wait for a settlement until the credit period expires or to offer a considerable purchase discount for an earlier remittance. Thus, in a sale amounting to $500, terms: 2/10, n/30, the seller must allow a purchase discount of $10 to receive the funds in 10 days or wait 30 days for the full amount. If the sale were subject to a 30-day trade acceptance, the transaction would still give the customer 30 days credit and would enable the seller to discount the paper, receiving $497.50, assuming the bank's rate of discount in this case to be 6 per cent per annum. The banks will advance cash more readily on a trade acceptance than on the seller's own note because the trade acceptance is a two-name paper; that is, the bank holds the acceptor primarily liable on his acceptance and the drawer liable on his indorsement. Another reason for the bank's preference for the trade acceptance is that it is selfliquidating; which means that it will be paid out of the proceeds realized from the sale of the merchandise purchased with the trade acceptance. 

Trade acceptances are recorded in the Notes Payable account of the acceptor and in the Notes Receivable account of the drawer, except where they are used so frequently that it is desirable to keep the information apart from the records of notes and other acceptances. In such cases, the acceptor carries a

special account called Trade Acceptances Payable and the drawer a special account called Trade Acceptances Receivable. 

The Certified Check 

When a payment is made by check, the payee has no absolute assurance that there are sufficient funds to the credit of the drawer's account at the bank to meet the check. To make certain that there are, it is necessary to have the cashier at the bank certify the check. He does this by writing across the face of the check "Certified" over his signature after making certain that there are sufficient funds in the depositor's account to cover the check. The words Accepted and Good are sometimes used. By certifying, the bank also guarantees that the drawer's signature on the check is genuine. Either the drawer or the holder of the check may have it certified. 

When a check is certified, the bank at once charges the amount against the depositor's account and credits a special account called Certification of Checks. The bank becomes liable for the payment of the check. When the check is finally paid, the certification account is debited.

Callis Hartley

Certification of a check is virtually an acceptance by the bank (the drawee), pledging to pay the holder (the payee) out of its own funds when the check is presented for payment. The certified check, while fundamentally a sight draft, resembles the time draft in procedure, since it is presented at the bank twice, once for acceptance, and once for payment. As far as the bookkeeping records are concerned, the certified check like the ordinary check is treated as a cash instrument.

DRAFTS 

Summary 

1. In a time draft the due date is found by counting the time from the date of the draft, or from the date of acceptance. 

2. The time draft is presented to the drawee first for acceptance, and later for payment. The drawee writes "Accepted" over his signature across the face of the draft. The date of acceptance need not be stated on a draft payable after date. It must be indicated on a draft payable after sight to fix the due date. 

3. The drawer's entry in the journal when the draft is accepted consists of a debit to Notes Receivable and a credit to the drawee's account. The drawee's entry in the journal consists of a debit to the drawer's account and a credit to 

Notes Payable. 

4. When the draft is paid, the drawer makes an entry in the Cash Receipts journal, crediting Notes Receivable. 

The drawee makes an entry in the Cash Payments journal, debiting Notes Payable. 

5. The trade acceptance is a special form of time draft given for the purchase of merchandise. Selling on trade acceptance is preferred by many firms over selling on account because they can discount these drafts readily. 

6. Certification of a check represents acceptance by the bank of the check drawn by the depositor. The bank certifies that the drawer's signature is genuine and agrees to pay the check when it is presented for payment.

(b) If you were the creditor, which terms would you prefer?

Why?

6. In what respect does a trade acceptance differ from an ordinary acceptance? 

7. A and B form a partnership to manufacture furniture. A feels that the firm should sell to reliable customers on account. B maintains that its sales on credit should be subject to trade acceptances. With which partner do you agree? Give reasons in support. of the side you take. 

8. A firm in need of funds decides to apply to its bank for a loan on its own note. It submits its balance sheet to the bank, showing trade acceptances on hand sufficient to cover the loan. The bank decides to make the loan on the trade acceptances instead of taking the firm's note. Explain why the bank shows a preference for the trade acceptances. 

9. Under what circumstances would you, as a dealer, insist that a check offered by a customer should be certified? 

10. Why does the bank, when it certifies a check, deduct the amount immediately from the depositor's account instead of doing so when the check comes through, as in the case of an ordinary check?

55. THE NOTE REGISTERS 

The occasional handling of a note or an acceptance in a business does not require any more detailed record than is furnished by the note accounts and the entries in the journals from which these accounts are constructed. Where, however, a business makes frequent use of notes and acceptances in its dealings with others, the data in the ledger and in the books of original entry are often inadequate to supply the information sought and are not conveniently arranged to afford easy reference. 

If, for example, a merchant has issued a considerable number of notes and acceptances, it becomes no easy matter for him to keep track of their maturities, the amounts due on those that are interest-bearing, the payee in each case, and the place where payment is to be made, if he has to rely on the records in the Notes Payable account or has to trace these back to their original entries. 

The particulars concerning notes and acceptances are set forth in two supplementary books known as note registers. The notes and acceptances receivable are recorded in the Notes Receivable Register, and the notes and acceptances payable are recorded in the Notes Payable Register. These registers are auxiliary records that supply the particulars about the negotiable paper held by the firm or issued by it. It should be noted that these two books do not take the place of the note accounts or the note entries in the journal. So that, when, for example, a note is received, it is entered in the journal, posted to the Notes Receivable account, and finally, recorded as a supplementary record in the Notes Receivable Register. 

Models of the note registers are shown on pages 398 and 399.

Some of the column headings require comment. (1) The column headed Maker or Acceptor in each book contains the name of the party primarily liable—the maker in the case of a note, and the acceptor in the case of an acceptance. (2) In the column headed 

Indorser or Drawer in the Notes Receivable Book, the indorser of a note or the drawer of a three-party acceptance is indicated. 

(3) The heading Date of Paper represents the date from which 

396

THE NOTE REGISTERS 

397 the time is reckoned for fixing the maturity of the paper. In a note or a draft payable after date it is the same as the date when the instrument is drawn. In a draft payable after sight it is the same as the date of acceptance. (4) The division of the space marked When Due into calendar months is for the purpose of showing at a glance what papers mature in any particular month. This facilitates prompt collection and payment. (5) The columns in the two books headed respectively 

Disposed Of and Redeemed give the particulars relating to the disposal of the notes receivable and the settlement of the notes payable. An inspection of these columns shows readily what paper is unpaid. The total amount of the open items in either register should agree with the balance shown in the corresponding note account in the ledger. Summary 

1. The note registers are not part of the double entry system but merely supplementary books of record. They give in convenient form detailed information concerning the notes and acceptances received or issued by the business. 

2. Each note or acceptance received after being entered in the journal and the Notes Receivable account, is recorded in detail in the Notes Receivable Register. Notes and accept ances given are similarly treated in the Notes Payable Register. 

1. What facts concerning a note receivable are given in the note register that are not already recorded in the journal and the note account? 

2. How may the accuracy of the balances in the Note accounts be tested where note registers are used. 

3. Explain why the terms "maker" and "acceptor" appear in the same column heading in each note register.

CHAPTER XIII BUSINESS PRACTICE 58. BANKING PROCEDURE 

A considerable amount of space has been given in preceding pages to describe and illustrate various phases of banking procedure that concern the depositor. Before proceeding with the concluding business practice set, it seems worthwhile to consider two additional topics on banking that should be clearly understood in a study of business procedure. The first of these deals with the practice of banks of returning the checks of the depositor with a statement of his account; the second with the question as to how a check issued by a depositor eventually reaches his bank for payment.

The Bank Reconciliation Statement 

The bank keeps an account with each depositor, crediting him with the funds and checks deposited, with the proceeds of notes and drafts left by the depositor for collection, and with bank interest to which the depositor may be entitled if his balance throughout the month has remained above a minimum amount prescribed by the bank. The depositor's account is debited with all checks drawn by him as they reach the bank, with all bank payments for notes and acceptances signed by the depositor, and with collection and exchange charges on paper collected by the bank or on out-of-town checks deposited with it. Each depositor's checks and other paper paid by the bank are kept in an individual folder and are returned marked "Paid" to the depositor when an accounting at the end of the month is made to him. The returned checks are known as canceled vouchers.

Two methods are employed by banks for rendering the monthly accounting to their depositors. The older method consists of balancing the pass book which the depositor is requested to hand 409

BUSINESS PRACTICE 

in at the end of the month. The amounts entered as deposits during the month are totaled and from the sum is subtracted the total sum paid, obtained by listing the amounts of the canceled checks on the adding machine. Most banks have done away with this method, furnishing the depositor, instead, with a bank statement, which is a transcript of his account, showing the balance at the beginning of the month, the debit and credit items entered during the month, and the balance at the end of the month. The depositor signs a receipt on delivery to him of the canceled vouchers and the bank statement. A typical bank statement is shown on the next page. 

The balance reported on the bank statement is not likely to agree with the balance shown on the stub in the check book, because some checks issued towards the end of the month that have been deducted in the check book may not have reached the bank for payment at the time the statement was prepared. The difference between the two balances must be accounted for before the bank statement and the check book record may be considered correct. The process of doing this is known as reconciling the bank statement with the check book record. The following procedure for making this reconciliation is suggested: (1) Arrange the canceled checks in numerical order. (2) Compare the amount of each check with the amount on the corresponding stub and check-mark each stub for which there is a canceled check. (3) 

Prepare from the check-stubs a list of the checks that have not been returned, giving the check numbers and the amounts. 

These checks are referred to as outstanding checks. (4) Deduct the total amount of the outstanding checks from the balance on the bank statement. If no error has been committed, the result will equal the check book balance. (5) Draw up a bank reconciliation statement on the page opposite to the last stub record for the month or on the back of the bank statement. A simple form of the bank reconciliation statement is shown on page 412.

It is apparent that while both balances reported in the reconciliation statement are correct the actual amount of cash against which additional checks may be drawn is represented by

In the foregoing illustration of the bank reconciliation the cause of the discrepancy between the bank account and the check book record is traced to the outstanding checks. Frequently the disagreement results from other causes as well. Thus, the bank statement may show a credit for bank interest or a debit for exchange charges for the month, for neither of which any record has been made in the check book. In such cases, it is obvious, the check book balance is no more a true statement of the status of the bank account than is the balance on the bank statement. It is necessary to adjust both balances to arrive at the true bank balance. The arrangement in the illustration on page 413 serves two purposes; it reconciles the two records and establishes the actual amount available for withdrawal. 

In this illustration the bank reports a balance of $3,268.80 without taking into account the three outstanding checks listed. 

The check book balance is reported as $3,032.10 without taking into account the bank interest credited by the bank or the exchange charged. The actual balance against which the depositor may issue checks is $3,043.70.
