The Clearing House 

It is interesting to know how a depositor's check is paid by his bank. The payee may present the check directly at the depositor's bank and, on proper identification, receive the cash. 

As a rule, this procedure is inconvenient to the payee and is rarely followed. Ordinarily the payee deposits the check at his own bank and the latter passes it on to the depositor's bank through a central banking agency known as a Clearing House. 

The course followed is illustrated in the diagram on page 414. 

In every fair-sized business center, the local banks have a mutual association which maintains a Clearing House, principally for the purpose of enabling each member bank to exchange checks deposited with it and drawn on the other member banks for checks' drawn against it and held by the other member banks. 

The process of making this exchange is known as clearing. 

A simplified case will illustrate how checks are cleared. 

There are three banks in a certain business center that exchange checks with one another through a clearing house. 

Each bank is identified by a clearing house number. At the close of business each day the clearing house clerk at each bank selects from the deposits for the day the checks drawn on the other two banks. He indorses these by stamping on the back "Paid through the . . . Clearing House," followed by the date, the name of the sending bank, and its clearing house number. He then assorts these checks into two

BUSINESS PRACTICE 

Drawer 

Drawer's Bank 

Clearing House Payee 

Payee's Bank groups, one for each bank, and places each set in an envelope. 

The clearing house number of the bank on which the checks are drawn and the total amount of the checks in the package are entered on the outside of the envelope. A sheet is prepared showing the total amount of checks against each bank. 

At a fixed hour the next day the clerks of the three banks meet at the clearing house and at a given signal of the clearing house manager exchange packages. The clearing house manager strikes a balance for each member bank by finding the difference between the total amount of the checks turned in by the bank and the total amount of checks delivered to it. If the amount turned in is greater than the amount received, the bank becomes for the time being a creditor bank. 

If the amount turned in is less than the amount of checks received, the bank is a debtor bank. At a stated hour later in the day each debtor bank makes a settlement with the clearing house, and each creditor bank receives payment from the clearing house for the balance due it. After these settlements have been made the clearing house is neither richer nor poorer than it was at the beginning of the day. Thus, the clearing house serves as the machinery for clearing the accounts among its member banks. The diagram on the opposite page illustrates the clearings of three banks. Bank A has checks amounting to $20,000, of which $12,000 represents the drafts on bank B and $8,000 on bank C. Bank B has checks amounting to $17,000, of which $10,000 represents the drafts

BANKING PROCEDURE 

415 on bank A and $7,000 on bank C. Bank C has checks amounting to $25,000, of which $9,000 represents drafts on bank A and $16,000 on bank C. 

As a result of the day's clearings, bank B makes a cash payment to the clearing house of $11,000, of which $1,000 is distributed to bank A and $10,000 to bank C.

Duc from Clearing House $1,000 

Due to Clearing House $11,000 

From Bunk A-$8,000 

From Bank B-$7,000

Bank Returned Checks $15,000 

Due from Clearing House $10,000

BUSINESS PRACTICE Summary 

1. At the end of the month the bank returns to each depositor his cancelled vouchers and a statement of account. 

2. Usually the check book balance and the bank statement balance do not agree because the two sets of records are not identical. 

3. A reconciliation statement must be prepared to account for the discrepancy and thereby establish the correctness of the check book and the bank statement records. 

4. Banks clear their checks daily through a central clearing house. Each bank surrenders the checks on other banks that have been deposited by its depositors and receives from other banks the checks drawn by its depositors. Settlement is made between each bank and the clearing house for the difference between the total amount of the checks turned in and the total amount of checks received by each bank. 

1. Give two reasons why the check book balance may not agree with the balance on the bank statement on the same day. 

2. State the steps to be followed in reconciling the check book and the bank statement. 

3. ( (a) When will the bank statement balance represent the amount available for further withdrawal? (b) When will the check book balance represent this amount? (c) When will this amount differ from both the check book and the bank statement balances. 

4. Explain what is meant by the statement in the lower left-hand corner in the following check:

BANKING PROCEDURE 

417 No. 320 

New York,.

Sept 18, 1926 

The Fifth Avenue Bank 

43rd Street and 5th Avenue 

Pay to the order of Charles Hayward - $125 

One Hundred twenty-five "f

PAYABLE THROUGH THE NEW YORK CLEARING HOUSE 

00 100 Dollars 

Sidney C. Woffson 

5. Describe the process of clearing and show its advantages over exchanging checks directly between banks.

BANKING PROCEDURE 

419 (a) Find the total amount turned in by each bank and the total amount drawn against each bank. (b) Name the debtor banks and state the amounts due to the clearing house. (c) Name the creditor banks and state the amounts due them by the clearing house. (d) Prove the amounts in your answers to b and c. 

14. Project for the Class-Problem: 

To Demonstrate the Operation of a Clearing House. 

One student should be appointed as clearing house manager with two assistants. The rest of the class should be broken up into teams of two. Each team is to represent a member bank of the clearing house association and is to receive a clearing house number. Each team is to prepare five packages of three checks each drawn on five other member banks. The clearing is to be made under the direction of the clearing house manager, each team having at the close a memorandum of the total amount turned in and the total amount of checks received. 

The balance should be struck by each team and countersigned by the manager. Teams of debtor banks should pay their balances to the clearing house manager, who, in turn should settle his indebtedness with creditor member banks.

59.  SET B-THE WHOLESALE SPECIALTY FURNITURE BUSINESS PART I 

The following set is designed to serve as a review of all the principles of elementary bookkeeping treated in this text, to furnish drill in making the bookkeeping records, and to afford practice in the use of business papers. The publishers have put up Outfit No. 2 which contains the incoming and the outgoing business forms for the set. The use of these papers is, however, optional. 

General Directions 

1. The following books are to be used: (1) a general journal, (2) a purchase journal, (3) a sales journal, (4) a four-column cash book, (5) a sales return and allowance journal, (6) a purchase return and allowance journal, (7) a ledger, (8) a trial balance book, (9) a notes receivable register, and (10) a notes payable register. 

2. Head the money columns in the cash book as follows: on the "Receipts" side, (1) Sundry Accounts, Cr., (2) Cash 

Sales, (3) Discount on Sales, (4) Net Cash; on the "Payments" 

side, (1) Sundry Accounts, Dr., (2) General Supplies and 

Services, (3) Discount on Purchases, and (4) Net Cash. 

3. Allow one third of a ledger page for each account. If this proves inadequate, forward the account to a new page. Label the accounts on each page a, b, and c. Thus, the accounts on page 1 will have the folio numbers 1a, 1b, and 1c. Prepare an alphabetical index and enter each account, as soon as it is opened, in the index, writing the name of the account and its folio number. Thus, if the cash account is the first one opened, it will appear under "C" in the index as "Cash . . . . la." 

4. Use ledger pages 1 to 10 inclusive for miscellaneous accounts, arranging the asset accounts, except accounts receivable, first, followed by the Notes Payable account, and finally the capital 420

accounts. The accounts receivable are to be kept on pages 11 to 14 inclusive, and the accounts payable on pages 15 and 16. 

5. Supply and service items are to be recorded in the following accounts: Freight and Cartage Out, Store Supplies and Services, Insurance, Rent, Salaries, Office Supplies, General Supplies and Services. and Collection and Exchange. 

6. The purchase and sales prices of the articles sold are given in the following price list. Refer to it in preparing the sales invoices and in computing the inventory when taking stock.

Dec. 6, claiming that a special price of $24 each on item # 782 had been quoted to him when the order was placed. Sent him a credit memorandum for $21. 

No. 70. The proprietor took 1 # 445, and 1 # 894 out of stock for home use.

NOTE: Figure these items at cost. 

No. 71. Received a check, Incoming Form 38, from B. Morgan for the sale of the following: 12 # 796, 6 # 576, 3 # 331. 

Tuesday, December 11, 19No. 72. Sold the bookkeeper 1 # 332 at cost and applied it against his salary for the week. 

Write the entry in the general journal.

No. 73. Mailed a check to the Colonial Art Co. in payment of invoice of Dec. 1 less 2%.

Follow the regular procedure. Complete the record on the invoice and transfer it to the proper file. 

No. 74. Paid $1.75 in currency to G. Carter for repairing a window pane.

Write out a receipt and have it signed. Charge to General Supplies and Services.

No. 75. Sold the Clover Art Shop, City, terms: 2/10, n/30, 10 # 445. 

Wednesday, December 12, 19No. 76. Received a credit slip from the bank for the collection of 

A. Baldwin's acceptance for $350, due Dec. 11.

Make the check stub record and the cash book entry, using Incoming Form 39. 

No. 77. Sent a check to Kohler & Rogers for the balance due on the invoice of Dec. 2 less 3%. 

No. 78. Received an order from R. Carson, Poughkeepsie, N. Y., for 6 # 344, 6 # 770. A postal money order for $84 was enclosed. 

Shipped the goods by freight. 

Prepare the straight bill of lading in triplicate and make out a receipted invoice. Make the entry from Incoming Form 40. 

Thursday, Dec. 13, 19No. 79. Left Benj. Walter's note (Schedule B, page 422) at the bank for collection.

BUSINESS PRACTICE 

No. 80. Received a check from H. J. Webb in payment of invoice of Dec. 2 less 2%. 

Refer to Incoming Form 41.

No. 81. Sold the Greater City Furniture Co., City, terms: 2/10, n/30, 12 # 770, 15 # 871, 8 # 886. 

No. 82. Gave a check for $52.50 to the Stevens Coal Co. for coal delivered to the proprietor's home. 

Friday, December 14, 19 

No. 83. Received a check for $249 from the Clover Art Shop for the balance on invoice of Nov. 28. 

Make the record from Incoming Form 42. 

No. 84. Deposited the four checks and the money order received since Dec. 10.

Prepare a proof of cash and the bank deposit. The latter should amount to $1025.86. 

No. 85. Paid $4.80 in currency for a dozen Mazda electric lamps bought of the Beacon Electric Co.

Enter on the currency record and write the receipt. Charge to 

General Supplies and Services 

No. 86. Gave M. Harrison a check for $95 for cartage of goods from Nov. 25 to date. The cartage charge on purchases amounted to $35; the balance was charged for deliveries to the city trade and for cartage to the freight yard on shipments to out-of-town customers. 

Saturday, December 15, 19No. 87. Received a credit slip from the bank for the collection of 

B. Walter's note for $420 with interest at 6% for 60 days.

Complete the note book record and write the entry from Incoming 

Form 43. 

No. 88. Mailed a check for $912 to the Western Furniture Co. in payment of the trade acceptance due today. 

Complete the note book record. 

No. 89. Drew a check for $13.50 for the balance of salary due to the bookkeeper.

No. 90. The proprietor, who had been contemplating going into a new line of business, received an attractive offer for the sale of his business which he decided to accept. Both parties to the transaction agreed that the sale should be made on Dec. 17. The bookkeeper was instructed to take the necessary steps for completing all the records and closing the books for the current period.

At the request of the proprietor, the bank furnished a statement of account from Dec. 1 to Dec. 15 inclusive. A charge of $4.45 not previously reported was included for collections made by the bank and for exchange on out-of-town checks. The two outstanding November checks, Nos. 8 and 10, and all the December checks except Nos. 

23 and 24 were returned with the statement. 

Enter the collection and exchange item on the check stub and in the cash journal. Prepare the bank reconciliation statement, using 

Incoming Form 44. 

No. 91. Close the special journals and bring the posting up to date. 

No. 92. Take a trial balance and compare the balances of the note accounts with the totals of the open items in the note books. 

No. 93. Stock was taken by the proprietor resulting in the following inventory of the items carried: 32 # 331 25 # 575 19 #782 48 # 332 20 # 576 22 #796 40 # 344 18 # 582 26 # 798 37 # 345 27 # 588 8 # 871 26 # 361 29 # 590 29 # 872 28 # 362 39 # 611 25 #885 25 # 441 32 # 624 15 # 886 22 # 445 38 #635 24 # 892 36 # 561 19 # 770 18 # 894 25 # 562 16# 774 Copy the foregoing inventory record on the stock sheet and make the extensions, using the cost price. The total value of the stock on hand should amount to $7522.

No. 94. Prepare the balance sheet and the statement of profit and loss from the data in the trial balance, the stock sheet and the following schedule of sundry inventories and appraisals: 

Furniture and Fixtures-book value less 1% depreciation.
